Table 3
Approval Matrix & Delegated Authority
1. Q: How would you teach staff to apply an approval matrix consistently?
A: Show answer
The approval matrix should translate delegated authority into simple decisions based on transaction type, amount, funding source, project, or other applicable criteria. Staff should learn that approval authority is not automatically transferable because a manager is unavailable; any temporary delegation should follow the organization’s documented authorization process, including scope and duration. Training should include examples below, at, and above approval thresholds, as well as transactions requiring special approval. The matrix should identify who may initiate, review, approve, and authorize payment and should distinguish budget approval from payment authorization. A controlled master matrix should be maintained so staff do not rely on outdated copies. This approach makes the control practical: staff should be able to look at a transaction, identify the applicable rule, confirm the authorized person, and document the evidence of approval.

Study instruction:
- Learn to apply the matrix as a decision tool rather than treating it as a list of names.

Key point:
- Approval authority should be defined by documented rules, not informal practice.
- Staff need to distinguish preparation, review, approval, and payment authorization.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
2. Q: What is an approval matrix in an NGO finance environment?
A: Show answer
An approval matrix is a controlled framework that specifies who has authority to approve particular transactions or decisions and under what conditions. It can include spending thresholds, transaction categories, donor restrictions, project requirements, procurement stages, and special approval requirements. A useful matrix does more than state that a manager can approve expenses; it defines the circumstances in which that authority applies. For example, a program manager may approve expenditure within an approved project budget up to a specified limit, while higher-value commitments may require country-director, finance, or board-level approval. The matrix should also distinguish between approving the underlying business decision and authorizing the related payment. That distinction prevents staff from assuming that a budget approval automatically means a payment is ready to be released.

Study instruction:
- Review how transaction type, threshold, funding conditions, and role interact within the matrix.

Key point:
- An approval matrix converts delegated authority into operational rules.
- Approval of a transaction and authorization of payment may be separate controls.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
3. Q: Why is an approval matrix important for NGO internal control?
A: Show answer
An approval matrix makes financial authority explicit, which helps prevent unauthorized expenditure and reduces ambiguity when several teams participate in a transaction. In an NGO, this is particularly important because spending may involve program managers, procurement staff, finance officers, country directors, regional offices, and donor-specific requirements. Without a defined approval structure, staff may rely on informal expectations or historical practice. A documented matrix also supports segregation of duties by separating the person who requests or prepares a transaction from the person who independently reviews and approves it. It provides evidence that the organization has established a consistent authorization process rather than allowing transactions to be approved on an ad hoc basis. Strong control design should also consider the organization’s size and operating model, because small offices may need compensating controls where complete separation of duties is not feasible.

Study instruction:
- Connect approval authority with authorization, segregation of duties, accountability, and risk management.

Key point:
- A clear matrix reduces ambiguity over who may authorize expenditure.
- Control design should reflect organizational risk and operating capacity.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
4. Q: What information should an approval matrix contain?
A: Show answer
At minimum, an approval matrix should identify the transaction or decision category, applicable thresholds, required approval roles, and any special conditions. Depending on the NGO, it may also include donor or funding restrictions, project ownership, procurement method, currency considerations, emergency procedures, and whether one or more approvals are required. It should be clear enough that a staff member can determine the required route without relying on personal interpretation. The matrix should also specify whether an approval can be delegated, whether dual approval is required, and what evidence must be retained. Names alone are less useful than positions or roles because staff turnover can make a person-based document obsolete. Where names are shown for practical use, they should be controlled through a master version that is updated promptly when responsibilities change.

Study instruction:
- Practice identifying the minimum fields needed to make an approval matrix usable at transaction level.

Key point:
- The matrix should define rules, roles, thresholds, and evidence requirements.
- Role-based authority is generally easier to maintain than a static list of individuals.

Source links:
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
5. Q: How would you explain approval thresholds to non-finance staff?
A: Show answer
Approval thresholds should be taught as control boundaries rather than as targets to be reached. A threshold does not mean a manager may spend up to that amount without considering budget availability, procurement requirements, donor restrictions, supporting documentation, or conflicts of interest. Staff should understand that the correct approver is determined by the transaction’s characteristics, not by convenience. Training should use realistic examples such as a purchase below a threshold, a purchase exactly at the threshold, and a purchase slightly above it. Examples should also show what happens when several related transactions are split into smaller amounts. The purpose is to prevent staff from treating thresholds mechanically while also making the decision process simple enough to apply consistently in daily operations.

Study instruction:
- Work through sample transactions below, at, and above each major approval threshold.

Key point:
- Thresholds define authority boundaries; they do not replace other financial controls.
- Staff should recognize situations where a transaction requires escalation.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
6. Q: How should staff handle transactions that are exactly at an approval threshold?
A: Show answer
A transaction that reaches an approval threshold should be treated according to the rule governing that threshold, without rounding, informal interpretation, or deliberate fragmentation. Training should use exact examples because staff sometimes remember only approximate limits. The organization should define whether the threshold applies to the individual transaction, purchase order, contract value, cumulative commitment, annual supplier relationship, or another basis. It should also clarify whether taxes, shipping, related costs, or optional extensions count toward the limit. These details matter because two transactions with the same invoice value can require different approvals depending on the organization’s policy. Finance should be able to explain the basis used and retain evidence supporting the approval route.

Study instruction:
- Verify what the threshold is actually calculated on before deciding who must approve a transaction.

Key point:
- Thresholds need a clearly defined calculation basis.
- Exact-threshold transactions should not be handled through informal rounding or assumptions.

Source links:
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
7. Q: How would you teach staff to identify the correct approver?
A: Show answer
The correct approver should be determined by the matrix rather than by who is available, senior, familiar, or willing to sign. Staff should first identify the transaction category, value, funding source, project ownership, and any special approval condition. They should then check the current matrix for the corresponding authority level. Training should emphasize that the person initiating or preparing a request does not automatically become the person who approves it. Staff should also understand when Finance is reviewing accounting treatment without becoming the operational owner of the expenditure. A simple decision tree can help: What is being purchased? Which project or funding source pays? What is the total relevant amount? Does a special rule apply? Which role has authority? What supporting evidence is required? This reduces guesswork and makes the control easier to audit.

Study instruction:
- Use a step-by-step decision tree to identify the required approver for sample transactions.

Key point:
- Approval should follow the matrix, not personal preference or availability.
- Finance review and operational approval should remain conceptually distinct.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
8. Q: What is the difference between initiating, reviewing, approving, and authorizing payment?
A: Show answer
These roles represent different points in the transaction control chain. The initiator identifies the need and prepares or requests the transaction. The reviewer checks completeness, accuracy, eligibility, or compliance. The approver makes the management decision that the transaction should proceed within delegated authority. Payment authorization is the confirmation that funds may actually be released, often after accounting and documentary checks are complete. In some small NGOs one person may perform more than one role because staffing is limited, but the overlap should be recognized and compensating controls considered. Staff should never assume that signing one document automatically satisfies every control requirement. Teaching these distinctions is important because many approval failures occur when employees use a single signature as proof that the whole process has been completed.

Study instruction:
- Map each role from request through payment and identify where responsibilities should be separated.

Key point:
- One signature does not necessarily replace review and payment controls.
- The control chain should be clear from initiation to final authorization.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- USAID OIG — Internal Control Review
9. Q: How should budget approval differ from payment authorization?
A: Show answer
Budget approval confirms that a spending plan or activity is authorized within the organization’s planning and governance framework. Payment authorization confirms that a specific payment is supported and may be released. These are related but different decisions. A budget may have been approved months earlier, but an individual payment still requires evidence that the goods or services were received, the amount is correct, the coding is appropriate, and the transaction complies with the relevant procurement and funding requirements. Staff should therefore avoid the statement that “it is in the budget, so it is approved.” Finance can support the distinction by designing forms and workflow stages that show budget availability separately from transaction approval and payment release. This prevents approved budgets from becoming automatic permission for unsupported payments.

Study instruction:
- Compare the evidence required for budget approval with the evidence required for payment release.

Key point:
- Budget authorization does not automatically authorize a specific payment.
- Transaction approval should include appropriate supporting evidence and control checks.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
10. Q: How should staff handle an approver who is absent?
A: Show answer
Staff should follow the documented delegation or acting-authority procedure rather than selecting an alternative approver informally. A valid temporary delegation should normally identify who is receiving the authority, what categories or thresholds are covered, when the delegation starts and ends, and whether any limitations apply. The evidence should be retained with the relevant governance or personnel records and should be available to Finance and reviewers. If no valid delegation exists, the transaction may need to wait, be escalated to the next authorized level, or use another documented route depending on the organization’s policy. Staff should not treat urgency as a reason to bypass the approval framework. Emergency procedures can exist, but they should themselves be documented and subject to later review.

Study instruction:
- Learn the organization’s formal temporary-delegation and emergency-approval procedures.

Key point:
- Absence does not automatically transfer authority.
- Temporary authority should be documented, limited, and traceable.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Delegated authorities
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
11. Q: What should happen when a manager asks staff to use someone else’s approval credentials?
A: Show answer
Staff should not use another person’s login, electronic signature, password, or approval credentials merely to keep a transaction moving. An approval is meaningful only when it can be attributed to the person authorized to make the decision. If the designated approver is unavailable, the organization should use its documented delegation process or another approved escalation route. Sharing credentials also weakens audit trails because the system may show one user while another person actually performed the action. The control issue therefore goes beyond technology. It affects accountability, authorization evidence, and the ability to investigate a disputed transaction. Finance and system administrators should make the permitted approval mechanisms clear and should ensure that workflow systems preserve the identity and role of the approving person.

Study instruction:
- Review the organization’s rules for electronic approvals, signatures, and user access.

Key point:
- Approval credentials should not be shared or substituted informally.
- Electronic approval controls should preserve user identity and accountability.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- USAID OIG — Internal Control Review
12. Q: How should an NGO handle dual-approval requirements?
A: Show answer
Dual approval should be designed so that two authorized people independently review the transaction rather than simply providing two signatures as a formality. The matrix should specify when dual approval is required, whether the two approvers must come from different functions, and whether one approval can be delegated. Examples may include high-value payments, bank transfers, unusual transactions, or sensitive changes to supplier or banking information. The supporting workflow should make it clear what each approver reviewed and when the approval occurred. Where dual approval is intended as a segregation-of-duties control, the two individuals should not simply replicate the same check without considering their respective responsibilities. An effective process makes the second approval a genuine additional control rather than a duplicate administrative step.

Study instruction:
- Identify which transactions require two approvals and what independent role each approver plays.

Key point:
- Dual approval should provide independent control value.
- The matrix should define when and how two-person authorization operates.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
13. Q: How would you prevent staff from splitting transactions to avoid a higher approval level?
A: Show answer
The control should focus on the economic substance and total commitment rather than only the individual invoice or purchase request. Training should show examples where several related orders form part of one requirement, contract, event, procurement exercise, or supplier commitment. Staff should know that deliberately breaking a transaction into smaller amounts to remain below an approval threshold defeats the purpose of the control. The organization should define how aggregation works and should require escalation when transactions are closely related. Finance and procurement can help by reviewing repeated purchases from the same supplier, similar requests raised within a short period, or multiple transactions associated with one activity. Automated reporting can flag patterns, but management review is still necessary because legitimate recurring purchases can look similar to intentional splitting.

Study instruction:
- Practice distinguishing legitimate separate purchases from related transactions that should be assessed together.

Key point:
- Approval thresholds should not be avoided by artificial transaction splitting.
- Review should consider related commitments and economic substance.

Source links:
- USAID — Procurement and Segregation of Duties Guidance
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
14. Q: What controls should apply to procurement approvals?
A: Show answer
Procurement approvals should connect the business need, budget availability, sourcing process, evaluation, award decision, receipt of goods or services, and payment. The same person should not normally control every stage of the process because separating authorization, custody, recording, and review reduces the risk of error or abuse. The approval matrix should therefore be aligned with procurement thresholds and should make clear which role approves the need, which role authorizes the procurement method or award, and which role approves payment after delivery. Staff also need to understand donor-specific procurement rules where applicable. A procurement approval is not complete merely because a purchase request has been signed; the organization should retain evidence showing that the relevant procurement process and authority requirements were followed.

Study instruction:
- Trace a procurement from request to payment and identify each required approval and control point.

Key point:
- Procurement approval should cover the full transaction cycle, not just the purchase request.
- Segregation of duties should be reflected in the workflow.

Source links:
- USAID — Procurement and Segregation of Duties Guidance
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
15. Q: How should donor restrictions affect an approval matrix?
A: Show answer
Donor restrictions can create approval requirements that are additional to the organization’s normal internal thresholds. A transaction may fall within a manager’s internal authority but still require donor approval, budget reallocation approval, no-cost extension authorization, or another documented donor-specific action. Staff should therefore check both layers: the organization’s delegated authority and the applicable funding conditions. The matrix or related donor-control schedule should identify these additional requirements clearly. Finance should avoid assuming that internal approval automatically satisfies external funding conditions. Training should include examples where a transaction is permissible under the organization’s authority but restricted by the grant agreement or approved budget. The control objective is to ensure that staff understand that authority to spend and eligibility to charge a donor are related but separate questions.

Study instruction:
- Compare internal authority rules with donor-specific restrictions before approving sample transactions.

Key point:
- Internal approval does not automatically establish donor eligibility.
- Funding-specific approvals should be visible in the control workflow.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
16. Q: How should approval authority relate to project codes and budget ownership?
A: Show answer
The person responsible for a project budget may need authority to approve certain expenditure, but project ownership does not automatically grant unlimited spending authority. The matrix should define how project managers interact with financial thresholds, donor restrictions, procurement procedures, and higher-level approvals. Finance should also distinguish between ownership of program results and authority over accounting treatment. A project manager may confirm that a cost is operationally necessary and belongs to a particular activity, while Finance determines whether the accounting classification and documentation are appropriate. When these responsibilities are made explicit, project managers can exercise meaningful budget accountability without being expected to perform technical accounting functions. The approval process should connect project codes to the correct budget owner while still preserving independent review where required.

Study instruction:
- Map project ownership, budget accountability, and financial approval roles separately.

Key point:
- Budget ownership does not create unlimited financial authority.
- Program and Finance responsibilities should complement rather than duplicate one another.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
17. Q: How would you train staff to recognize when special approval is required?
A: Show answer
Staff should learn that certain transactions can require additional authorization even when the amount is relatively small. Special cases may include related-party transactions, unusual contractual commitments, restricted funds, conflicts of interest, asset disposals, changes to bank details, exceptional procurement methods, emergency expenditure, or transactions outside the approved budget. The matrix should point staff to the relevant policy whenever a special condition is triggered. Training should use scenario exercises because special approval rules are often missed when employees focus only on the monetary threshold. A helpful approach is to teach staff to ask two questions: “What is the value?” and “What is unusual about the transaction?” The second question helps identify circumstances where a normal threshold-based approval is insufficient.

Study instruction:
- Build a checklist of special conditions that override or add to normal approval routes.

Key point:
- Monetary thresholds are only one dimension of approval authority.
- Unusual or sensitive transactions may require additional review.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
18. Q: How should emergency expenditure be handled without weakening the approval system?
A: Show answer
An NGO may need to spend urgently during emergencies, but urgency should trigger a defined alternative control route rather than eliminate controls. The emergency procedure should state who can authorize the expenditure, what evidence is required at the time, what limits apply, and when retrospective review must occur. Staff should understand that a transaction can be urgent without being undocumented. Where normal segregation is temporarily impossible, management should identify compensating controls such as post-payment review, independent reconciliation, exception reporting, or senior oversight. The emergency procedure should also be tested before a crisis occurs so staff know exactly how it works. This allows the organization to respond quickly while preserving accountability for the use of funds.

Study instruction:
- Review the emergency approval procedure and identify both immediate and post-event controls.

Key point:
- Emergencies may alter the workflow but should not eliminate accountability.
- Compensating controls can address temporary limitations in normal segregation.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- USAID OIG — 2025 Financial Statement Audit Recommendations
19. Q: How should staff document evidence of approval?
A: Show answer
Approval evidence should show who approved the transaction, what was approved, when the approval occurred, and, where relevant, the authority under which the person acted. The evidence can be a controlled workflow record, electronic approval, signed form, or other method authorized by policy. The important issue is traceability. A message saying “please proceed” may be inadequate if it cannot be linked clearly to the transaction and the authorized person. Finance should retain the approval together with the relevant supporting documentation or maintain a system reference that makes the evidence easy to retrieve. Training should emphasize that documentation is part of the control itself, not an administrative task that can be added after payment. This becomes particularly important when the transaction is later reviewed by management, internal audit, external audit, or a donor.

Study instruction:
- Practice checking approval evidence for identity, authority, timing, scope, and linkage to the transaction.

Key point:
- A control is stronger when the approval evidence is clear and retrievable.
- Documentation should be retained as part of the transaction record.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
20. Q: What should Finance do when an approval appears to come from the wrong person?
A: Show answer
Finance should pause the transaction and determine whether the approver actually held authority at the relevant date and under the applicable matrix. The review should consider whether there was a valid delegation, temporary acting arrangement, special approval, or system configuration that explains the apparent discrepancy. If the evidence does not support the approval, the transaction should be escalated for correction rather than silently accepted because the expenditure itself appears reasonable. Finance should document the issue and, where appropriate, report repeated cases to management so the underlying process can be corrected. The purpose is not merely to reject transactions. It is to ensure that authorization evidence remains aligned with the organization’s documented authority structure.

Study instruction:
- Learn how to verify an approver’s authority at the transaction date rather than relying on current job titles.

Key point:
- The validity of approval depends on authority at the time of the decision.
- Exceptions should be documented and corrected through the proper process.

Source links:
- USAID OIG — Internal Control Review
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
21. Q: How should an approval matrix be kept current when staff roles change?
A: Show answer
The approval matrix should have a defined owner and a formal update process triggered by changes in organizational structure, staff appointments, resignations, acting assignments, or revised delegation limits. Updates should be reviewed and approved under the organization’s governance process rather than edited casually by individual users. A controlled version number and effective date make it possible to determine which matrix applied when a transaction was approved. Systems such as electronic workflows should also be aligned with the master matrix so that the documented policy and the system configuration do not diverge. Training should tell staff where the current version is stored and how to recognize an outdated copy. This prevents employees from using old authority levels after management responsibilities have changed.

Study instruction:
- Trace how a change in management responsibility should flow from policy to the controlled matrix and then to system access.

Key point:
- The matrix needs ownership, version control, and an effective date.
- Policy, matrix, and system permissions should remain aligned.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- USAID OIG — Internal Control Review
22. Q: Who should own the approval matrix?
A: Show answer
Ownership should be assigned to a role with sufficient authority to coordinate Finance, program management, procurement, human resources, and senior leadership requirements. The owner does not necessarily need to approve every threshold but should be responsible for maintaining the document, coordinating changes, confirming effective dates, and ensuring that relevant users are informed. Finance can provide technical control input, while senior management or the governing authority may retain responsibility for approving the delegation framework itself. The key is to avoid a situation where everyone assumes someone else maintains the matrix. The organization should also define who is responsible for updating system permissions when the approved matrix changes.

Study instruction:
- Identify the policy owner, approval authority, document custodian, and system-access owner for your organization.

Key point:
- Matrix ownership should be explicit rather than assumed.
- Updating the document and updating system permissions are related but distinct responsibilities.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
23. Q: How would you train new staff on approval authority?
A: Show answer
Training should happen before a new staff member is expected to approve or process transactions independently. The introduction should cover the organization’s authority structure, relevant thresholds, prohibited practices, documentation standards, delegation rules, and escalation routes. Rather than using only a policy document, Finance should use practical examples from the employee’s role. A program manager may need to approve activity expenditure, while a finance officer may need to review coding and documentation without becoming the operational approver. Training should include a short knowledge check and a clear explanation of where the current matrix is stored. Refresher training should be provided after major policy changes and may be appropriate periodically for staff with approval responsibilities.

Study instruction:
- Develop role-specific approval exercises rather than relying only on policy reading.

Key point:
- Approval training should be practical and role-specific.
- Staff should know where to find the current matrix before they begin approving transactions.

Source links:
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
24. Q: How can scenario-based training improve approval-matrix compliance?
A: Show answer
Scenario-based training allows staff to practice the decisions they will actually face. Examples should include routine purchases, high-value commitments, restricted-fund expenditure, urgent payments, absent approvers, transactions exactly at thresholds, related purchases, and requests where the documentation is incomplete. Participants should explain not only who approves but also why that person has authority and what evidence must accompany the transaction. The trainer can then introduce exceptions that require staff to reconsider their first answer. This approach helps reveal misunderstandings that a simple policy presentation may not uncover. The scenarios should be updated when policies, thresholds, donor conditions, or organizational structures change so that the training remains connected to current practice.

Study instruction:
- Use realistic transaction scenarios and require learners to explain the control reasoning behind each approval decision.

Key point:
- Scenario training tests whether staff can apply rules, not merely remember them.
- Scenarios should cover normal cases and exceptions.

Source links:
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
25. Q: What are common mistakes staff make when applying approval matrices?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include using an outdated matrix, choosing an approver based on availability rather than authority, confusing budget approval with payment authorization, ignoring donor-specific requirements, splitting related transactions, accepting verbal approval without proper evidence, and assuming that seniority automatically grants approval rights. Another frequent error is treating a familiar transaction as routine without checking whether its circumstances have changed. For example, a normal purchase may require special approval if it involves a related party or restricted funding. These mistakes are often process failures rather than accounting errors. Training should therefore focus on decision points and evidence requirements, while management should monitor recurring exceptions to determine whether the matrix, workflow, or staff training needs improvement.

Study instruction:
- Review recent approval exceptions and classify them by training, policy, workflow, or documentation failure.

Key point:
- Many approval failures arise from process misunderstanding rather than arithmetic errors.
- Exception patterns can identify where controls need strengthening.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- USAID OIG — 2025 Financial Statement Audit Recommendations
26. Q: How should staff respond when an approver gives verbal approval?
A: Show answer
Verbal approval may be part of an emergency or informal communication process, but staff should follow the organization’s rules for converting that approval into appropriate evidence. The key question is whether the organization recognizes the communication method as valid and whether the authority of the person giving the instruction can be demonstrated. Where policy requires written or system approval, staff should obtain that evidence before processing the transaction unless a documented emergency exception applies. A verbal instruction should not be treated as a permanent substitute for the formal control simply because the manager is senior or the transaction is urgent. Finance should define when verbal approvals are permitted, who may provide them, what documentation is required afterward, and who must review the exception.

Study instruction:
- Learn the organization’s rules for verbal, email, and electronic approvals and the required evidence for each.

Key point:
- Approval evidence must follow the organization’s authorized process.
- Emergency communication should be converted into a documented control trail where required.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
27. Q: How should approval controls work in a small NGO where there are few staff?
A: Show answer
Small organizations may not have enough staff to separate every activity across different people. That does not eliminate the need for control; it requires management to identify where duties overlap and establish practical compensating controls. For example, one person may prepare and record transactions while another person reviews bank reconciliations, payment listings, or exception reports. Senior management or trustees may perform periodic independent reviews of sensitive transactions. The organization should document these arrangements rather than assuming that everyone understands the informal process. The approval matrix should reflect actual staffing capacity and should not prescribe an impossible segregation model. The objective is reasonable control over authorization, recording, custody, and review, with additional monitoring where full separation is not feasible.

Study instruction:
- Map the actual duties of a small finance team and identify where compensating reviews are required.

Key point:
- Limited staffing may require compensating controls rather than abandonment of segregation principles.
- The control arrangement should be documented and reviewed periodically.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- USAID OIG — 2025 Financial Statement Audit Recommendations
28. Q: How should finance staff test whether an approval is within budget?
A: Show answer
Finance should distinguish between the authority to approve a transaction and the availability of budget. A manager may have authority to approve a transaction but still need to demonstrate that sufficient approved budget remains, that the expense is correctly charged to the project or cost center, and that donor restrictions are satisfied. A useful review therefore checks the approved budget, year-to-date expenditure, commitments where relevant, forecast requirements, and any approved budget revisions. Finance should avoid treating a current budget balance as automatic evidence that the expenditure is appropriate. The budget test is one part of the wider control chain. Clear reporting can help managers understand the financial consequence of approval decisions before commitments become difficult to reverse.

Study instruction:
- Practice reviewing approval authority together with budget availability, commitments, and funding restrictions.

Key point:
- Authorization and budget availability are connected but separate control questions.
- Managers should understand the future financial effect of their approvals.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
29. Q: What should happen when a transaction exceeds the approver’s authority after the transaction has already been prepared?
A: Show answer
The transaction should be routed to the appropriate higher authority rather than being processed under the lower threshold. Preparation does not create approval rights. The staff member should update the workflow or documentation so that the correct approver is clearly identified, and Finance should retain evidence of the escalation. If the transaction exceeds the threshold because additional costs were added later, the organization should assess whether the revised total requires a new approval before commitment or payment. Repeated cases may indicate that staff are not understanding the threshold rules or that the workflow is poorly designed. Management should treat this information as a process-improvement signal rather than simply correcting each transaction independently.

Study instruction:
- Practice escalating transactions when the final commitment moves into a higher approval band.

Key point:
- Authority is determined by the relevant transaction value and conditions.
- Later changes can trigger additional approval requirements.

Source links:
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
30. Q: How should changes to an approved transaction be handled?
A: Show answer
Material changes to an approved transaction should be reassessed rather than automatically covered by the original approval. The organization should define what constitutes a material change, such as increased value, different supplier, changed scope, different funding source, altered terms, or significant delay. Depending on the policy, minor administrative changes may not require reapproval, while substantive changes may need approval at the appropriate authority level. The workflow should preserve the original decision and the revised information so reviewers can see what changed. This prevents the approval process from becoming meaningless after the transaction evolves. Staff should understand that approval applies to the transaction as described, and significant changes can create a new decision that requires consideration.

Study instruction:
- Identify which changes require reapproval under the organization’s policies.

Key point:
- Approval should remain connected to the transaction actually being executed.
- Significant changes may require a fresh review or higher authorization.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
31. Q: How should approval controls apply to contracts and long-term commitments?
A: Show answer
Contract approval should consider the total commitment and the duration of the obligation, not merely the value of the first invoice. A contract may create financial exposure over several months or years even when individual payments remain below a manager’s approval threshold. The matrix should therefore specify whether authority is based on total contract value, annual value, expected spend, or another defined measure. Staff should also check procurement rules, legal review requirements, donor conditions, and budget availability. The person approving the contract should understand the financial and operational consequences of the commitment. Finance can support this by maintaining a commitments schedule that allows management to see obligations that have been authorized but not yet paid.

Study instruction:
- Compare invoice-level approval with total contractual commitment and identify the appropriate authority basis.

Key point:
- Long-term commitments should be assessed on the organization’s defined commitment basis, not only on individual invoices.
- Approval should consider future financial exposure.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
32. Q: How should payment approval be controlled when the purchase was already approved?
A: Show answer
Payment should still pass through the organization’s payment controls because approval of the purchase does not prove that the supplier’s invoice is accurate or that the goods or services were received. The payment review should consider the approved commitment, invoice, evidence of receipt or service, correct accounting and project coding, tax requirements where applicable, and any changes to supplier details. The person releasing payment should have the authority defined in the payment matrix and should not simply assume that an earlier operational approval is sufficient. Where payment is made by bank transfer, dual authorization or another compensating control may apply depending on the organization’s design. This layered approach helps detect errors between procurement and settlement.

Study instruction:
- Trace an approved purchase through invoice review and final payment authorization.

Key point:
- Purchase approval and payment authorization address different risks.
- Payment controls should verify that the approved commitment is being settled correctly.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
33. Q: How should approval authority be handled for payroll changes?
A: Show answer
Payroll changes need clear separation between the person requesting or preparing the change, the person authorized to approve it, and the person processing payroll where feasible. Examples include new hires, salary changes, allowances, bonuses, termination dates, bank-detail changes, and adjustments. The approval matrix should specify the appropriate authority for each category because not every payroll item carries the same level of risk. HR may validate employment information, management may approve personnel actions, and Finance or payroll staff may verify the financial and processing aspects. Sensitive changes should be supported by controlled documentation and appropriate review. The goal is to prevent unauthorized changes from entering payroll while maintaining a clear record of who made the underlying decision.

Study instruction:
- Separate personnel authorization, payroll processing, and financial verification when reviewing payroll changes.

Key point:
- Payroll approval should reflect the sensitivity and nature of the change.
- User access and approval rights should align with documented responsibilities.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- USAID OIG — Internal Control Review
34. Q: How should approval controls apply to supplier bank-detail changes?
A: Show answer
Supplier bank-detail changes should normally receive more control attention than routine invoice approvals because changing payment instructions can redirect funds even when the underlying invoice is legitimate. The organization should define who may request the change, who independently verifies it, who approves the update, and who has system access to modify the supplier record. Verification may include an approved independent confirmation process according to organizational policy. Finance staff should not treat a request in an email as sufficient evidence merely because it appears to come from a familiar contact. The approval matrix should therefore work together with master-data controls. Staff should understand that authority to approve an invoice does not automatically grant authority to alter supplier master records.

Study instruction:
- Review the separate approval and verification steps for supplier-master changes.

Key point:
- Supplier master-data changes can require stronger controls than routine payments.
- Approval authority for invoices should not be confused with system authority to amend vendor records.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- USAID OIG — Internal Control Review
35. Q: How should related-party transactions be handled in an approval matrix?
A: Show answer
Related-party transactions should be subject to the organization’s conflict-of-interest and governance requirements in addition to normal financial approval thresholds. An individual with a personal or organizational connection to the supplier or beneficiary should not simply approve the transaction because it falls within their monetary authority. The workflow should require disclosure of the relationship and independent review by the appropriate person or body. Depending on the organization’s policy, additional documentation or governance approval may be required. Staff should be trained to recognize potential conflicts even when the transaction price appears reasonable. The important control principle is that approval authority should not override independence requirements. The matrix should identify these cases clearly so staff know when the normal route is not sufficient.

Study instruction:
- Review the conflict-of-interest process alongside the approval matrix and identify when independent approval is required.

Key point:
- Normal spending authority may be restricted by conflict-of-interest rules.
- Independence should be preserved where an approver has a relevant interest.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
36. Q: How should approval matrices support segregation of duties?
A: Show answer
The approval matrix should be designed as part of the wider segregation-of-duties framework rather than as a standalone authorization table. It should show who initiates a transaction, who reviews it, who approves it, who records it, who handles related assets or cash, and who performs independent reconciliation or monitoring. The exact separation will vary with organizational size and system design. The key risk is allowing one individual to control too many stages of a transaction without compensating review. For example, if one person can create a supplier, approve a payment, and release funds, the approval matrix alone may not provide adequate protection. Staff should therefore learn to look beyond signatures and consider the complete chain of responsibilities.

Study instruction:
- Draw the end-to-end transaction flow and mark each role responsible for initiation, approval, recording, custody, and review.

Key point:
- Approval authority is only one component of segregation of duties.
- Effective controls consider the full transaction lifecycle.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- USAID — Procurement and Segregation of Duties Guidance
37. Q: How should system permissions align with the approval matrix?
A: Show answer
The system should not give users authority that exceeds the organization’s documented delegation. If the approval matrix says a role can approve only certain transaction types or amounts, the workflow system should be configured to reflect those boundaries where technically possible. System access reviews should occur when staff move roles, leave the organization, take temporary assignments, or receive expanded responsibilities. Finance, human resources, and system administrators may each have different responsibilities for maintaining the access environment, so the process should specify who requests, approves, implements, and reviews changes. A mismatch between policy and system access creates a control gap because a user may technically be able to approve transactions that the policy does not authorize them to approve.

Study instruction:
- Compare user permissions against the current approval matrix and identify any differences.

Key point:
- System capability should reflect approved authority.
- Access rights should be reviewed when roles or delegations change.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- USAID OIG — Internal Control Review
38. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, or VBA support approval-matrix controls?
A: Show answer
Automation can help enforce the matrix without replacing management judgment. A controlled Excel model can store the master approval rules, transaction types, thresholds, roles, and effective dates. Power Query can consolidate transaction data so Finance can identify items that appear to require escalation. VBA can flag transactions above thresholds, compare the assigned approver with the expected approval role, create exception reports, and record workflow status. However, automation should not invent authority or override documented policy. The source matrix must remain controlled, and exceptions should be routed for human review. A useful workbook might show transaction amount, project, donor, approval category, required approver, actual approver, approval date, and exception status. This turns the matrix into an operational control rather than merely a document stored in a policy folder.

Study instruction:
- Design a simple control sheet that compares each transaction against the approved authority rules.

Key point:
- Automation can identify approval exceptions and improve monitoring.
- The underlying authority rules must remain controlled and human-governed.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool
39. Q: How should Finance monitor approval-matrix exceptions?
A: Show answer
Finance should maintain an exception report that identifies transactions where approval was missing, delayed, outside the expected authority, documented incorrectly, or otherwise inconsistent with policy. The report should distinguish genuine control failures from legitimate exceptions supported by documented delegation or emergency procedures. Each exception should have a status, responsible owner, corrective action, and resolution date. Repeated exceptions should be analyzed for patterns rather than simply cleared one by one. For example, repeated transactions routed to the wrong manager may indicate that the matrix is confusing or that system rules have not been updated. Exception reporting therefore serves both compliance and process improvement. The aim is to make control weaknesses visible early enough for management to correct them.

Study instruction:
- Build an exception log that records the issue, root cause, corrective action, owner, and closure date.

Key point:
- Exception reporting supports both control monitoring and process improvement.
- Repeated exceptions may indicate a structural weakness rather than isolated staff error.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- USAID OIG — 2025 Financial Statement Audit Recommendations
40. Q: How should management review whether the approval matrix is still fit for purpose?
A: Show answer
Management should periodically assess whether the matrix still reflects the organization’s structure, transaction volume, risk profile, donor requirements, and operating environment. Changes in programs, geographic expansion, inflation, new funding arrangements, restructuring, or increased use of electronic payment systems can make old thresholds or responsibilities inappropriate. The review should examine transaction exceptions, audit findings, policy changes, staffing levels, and areas where approvals are causing unnecessary delays or bypass behavior. The purpose is not simply to increase thresholds. Management should consider whether the control remains capable of preventing or detecting unauthorized transactions while remaining practical for staff. The review and resulting changes should be documented, approved, communicated, and given a clear effective date.

Study instruction:
- Link matrix reviews to organizational change, risk assessment, audit findings, and recurring exceptions.

Key point:
- Approval authority should evolve with organizational risk and structure.
- Changes should be documented and communicated before they become operational.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
41. Q: How should approval controls be communicated to senior management and the board?
A: Show answer
Senior management and the board should understand the authority framework at a level appropriate to their oversight responsibilities. They do not need to review every transaction, but they should know which decisions are delegated, which remain reserved for higher authority, how exceptions are monitored, and what significant control issues have arisen. Finance can provide a concise summary of the current matrix, major changes, recurring exceptions, and unresolved approval weaknesses. Where authority is formally delegated by governing bodies, changes should be presented through the organization’s established governance process. Clear reporting helps oversight bodies understand whether management is operating within approved authority and whether the control framework remains appropriate for the organization’s size and risk profile.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a management-level summary showing delegation levels, major changes, and significant exceptions rather than transaction-level detail.

Key point:
- Oversight bodies need visibility over delegated authority and significant exceptions.
- Governance reporting should focus on authority, risk, and control effectiveness.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
42. Q: How would you handle disagreement between Finance and a manager about who has approval authority?
A: Show answer
The disagreement should be resolved by reference to the current approved policy and matrix rather than by hierarchy or personal interpretation. Finance should identify the specific rule, threshold, role, or delegation issue involved and show the supporting evidence. If the policy is genuinely ambiguous, the transaction should be escalated to the designated authority for interpretation and the outcome documented. Finance should avoid creating a one-off exception that is not reflected in the policy because this can create inconsistent treatment for future transactions. Where the same ambiguity recurs, the matrix should be clarified and communicated to staff. This approach keeps the discussion focused on governance and evidence rather than turning the disagreement into a personal conflict between Finance and the program team.

Study instruction:
- Practice resolving authority disputes by identifying the governing rule, evidence, and escalation route.

Key point:
- Policy interpretation should be documented when the matrix is unclear.
- Repeated disputes are a signal that the control framework needs clarification.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
43. Q: How should Finance distinguish a valid delegation from informal approval by a senior person?
A: Show answer
A valid delegation should be supported by the organization’s established authority framework and should identify the scope of authority being transferred. Informal instructions such as “the director told me to approve this” should not automatically be treated as equivalent to documented delegation. Finance should check whether the person giving or receiving the authority was permitted to delegate, whether the delegation was effective at the relevant date, and whether it covered the transaction category and amount involved. The evidence should be retained in an accessible form. This distinction matters because organizations can otherwise develop an informal shadow hierarchy where staff rely on personal relationships instead of formal authority. A controlled delegation process protects both the organization and the individuals involved.

Study instruction:
- Compare documented delegation requirements with informal approval examples and identify the difference.

Key point:
- Authority should flow through documented governance arrangements.
- Informal seniority should not substitute for a valid delegation.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Delegated authorities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
44. Q: How should an NGO review approval-matrix compliance after transactions have been processed?
A: Show answer
Periodic post-transaction review can test whether the approval matrix is working in practice rather than only on paper. A sample of transactions can be checked for the correct approver, valid delegation, required supporting documents, threshold treatment, budget authorization, and compliance with special conditions. The review can be risk-based, giving greater attention to high-value, unusual, sensitive, or exception-prone transactions. Findings should be documented and categorized so management can distinguish isolated mistakes from recurring control weaknesses. Where a control deficiency is identified, the organization should define corrective action and follow up on whether the issue was resolved. Post-transaction testing is especially useful because staff may comply with a workflow during training but develop shortcuts later under operational pressure.

Study instruction:
- Design a sample-based review that tests actual approval evidence against the current matrix.

Key point:
- Compliance should be tested using real transaction evidence.
- Follow-up should focus on whether identified weaknesses are corrected.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
45. Q: What would you do if staff repeatedly bypass the approval matrix because the process is too slow?
A: Show answer
Repeated bypassing should be treated as evidence that the control design or workflow may need review, not simply as a reason to ignore the policy. Management should first understand where the delay occurs: unclear authority, excessive approval layers, unavailable approvers, poor system configuration, incomplete documentation, or unnecessary manual steps. The organization can then simplify legitimate routine transactions while preserving stronger controls for higher-risk cases. For example, recurring low-risk transactions may use standardized workflows, while sensitive or high-value transactions retain additional review. Any change to the matrix should be formally approved and communicated rather than allowing informal shortcuts to become accepted practice. The aim is a control environment that staff can follow consistently without weakening authorization discipline.

Study instruction:
- Investigate the root cause of repeated bypasses before changing thresholds or approval requirements.

Key point:
- Persistent non-compliance may indicate a poorly designed process.
- Simplification should be controlled and formally approved rather than achieved through informal workarounds.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
46. Q: How should an NGO handle approval requirements when several funding sources are involved in one activity?
A: Show answer
When an activity is financed from multiple funding sources, the approval process should consider both the organization’s internal authority and the conditions attached to each funding source. A transaction may be operationally approved by a program manager, yet the allocation between funds may require additional review because different budgets or donor restrictions apply. Finance should verify the funding split, eligibility, available budget, and any donor-specific restrictions before final posting or payment. The approval matrix can include fields for funding source and special approval status so the workflow does not rely on memory. Staff should understand that a single activity does not necessarily mean a single approval route. The more funding sources involved, the more important clear documentation becomes.

Study instruction:
- Practice identifying how mixed funding changes the approval and financial-review requirements.

Key point:
- Funding-source complexity can create additional approval requirements.
- One operational decision may need several financial eligibility checks.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
47. Q: How should approval-matrix training address accountability without making non-finance managers responsible for technical accounting?
A: Show answer
Managers should be accountable for decisions that fall within their role, such as confirming business need, project relevance, budget responsibility, receipt of services, and compliance with operational requirements. They should not be expected to determine every technical accounting treatment themselves. Finance remains responsible for providing accounting expertise, reviewing classification, and explaining financial implications. Training should therefore make the boundary explicit: the manager owns the operational decision and the financial consequences within their authority, while Finance supports the accounting and control aspects. This distinction helps avoid two opposite problems: managers approving transactions without understanding their accountability, or Finance becoming the de facto operational decision-maker. Clear roles improve both control and cooperation because each function understands what it is expected to review.

Study instruction:
- Map which approval decisions belong to managers and which technical accounting decisions remain with Finance.

Key point:
- Accountability should follow the decision-maker’s role.
- Finance provides technical support without taking over operational ownership.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
48. Q: How should staff know when to escalate an approval issue to Finance or senior management?
A: Show answer
Escalation should occur when the transaction falls outside the staff member’s authority, the matrix is unclear, required evidence is missing, a donor or policy condition may be breached, a conflict of interest exists, the transaction appears unusual, or the normal segregation of duties cannot be maintained. Finance should define a clear escalation route so staff do not simply send difficult cases to the most senior available person. The escalation should document the issue, relevant transaction details, the rule that may apply, and the decision required. Where senior management provides a determination, the outcome should be recorded so similar cases can be handled consistently in the future. A good escalation process is therefore both a control and a learning mechanism for the organization.

Study instruction:
- Build an escalation checklist based on authority, documentation, donor restrictions, unusual circumstances, and segregation risks.

Key point:
- Staff should escalate defined exceptions rather than improvising approval solutions.
- Documented decisions create useful precedent for future cases.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
49. Q: How should an NGO measure whether staff are applying the approval matrix consistently?
A: Show answer
Consistency can be assessed through practical indicators such as the percentage of sampled transactions with the correct approver, the number of undocumented delegations, repeated approval exceptions, transactions routed to the wrong authority level, and the time taken to resolve exceptions. Training effectiveness can also be tested through scenario assessments before and after instruction. The organization should avoid treating “zero exceptions” as the only measure because some exceptions may be legitimate and properly documented. Instead, management should look at whether the control process identifies exceptions, resolves them appropriately, and prevents recurring weaknesses. Trends can be reported monthly or quarterly depending on transaction volume and risk. The goal is to determine whether employees can apply the matrix reliably in real operating conditions.

Study instruction:
- Develop a small set of approval-control indicators that measure both compliance and recurring exceptions.

Key point:
- Control effectiveness is measured through actual transaction behavior, not policy existence alone.
- Exception trends can reveal training and process weaknesses.

Source links:
- U.S. GAO — Green Book
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
50. Q: How would you turn an approval matrix into a practical daily control for NGO staff?
A: Show answer
The matrix becomes useful when it is integrated into the transaction workflow rather than left as a policy document. A practical process might begin with a standardized request form containing project, funding source, amount, transaction type, and requester. The workflow then identifies the expected approval level, checks whether additional conditions apply, routes the request to the authorized approver, and retains the approval evidence. Finance can review exceptions before payment and periodically test whether the actual workflow matches the policy. A controlled Excel model, Power Query process, or financial system can support these checks, but the organization should keep the authority rules centrally governed and clearly documented. Staff training should reinforce one simple principle: identify the transaction, check the current authority rule, obtain the required approval, preserve the evidence, and escalate anything outside the normal path.

Study instruction:
- Practice the complete workflow from transaction request through approval, documentation, payment control, and exception review.

Key point:
- An approval matrix is most effective when embedded in everyday workflow and monitoring.
- Consistent application depends on clear rules, current documentation, evidence, training, and escalation.

Source links:
- Charity Commission — Internal financial controls for charities
- U.S. GAO — Standards for Internal Control
- USAID — Organizational Capacity Assessment Tool

Table 4
Finance Checklist Training
1. Q: How would you train staff to use checklists without turning control procedures into mechanical box-ticking?
A: Show answer
Checklists should support professional review rather than replace it. Staff need to understand what each checklist question means, what evidence is acceptable, and what action is required when the evidence is incomplete or contradictory. For example, checking “approval obtained” should mean verifying that the authorized person approved the actual transaction, within the appropriate authority level, before the commitment or payment occurred. A signature by itself is not sufficient if the signer lacked delegated authority or approved a different document. Training should include examples where every box appears complete but the transaction is still problematic. This teaches staff to consider substance, timing, authorization, completeness, and reliability of evidence. Staff should also understand which matters they can resolve themselves and which require escalation to Finance management. Power Query can help prepare consistent control populations for review, while VBA can produce exception lists and record completion status, but neither tool should replace professional judgment.

Send to the team:
- “Please use each checklist question as a control test, not simply as a box to tick.”
Key point:
- A completed checklist is not automatically evidence of a sound transaction.
- Staff must understand what each control is testing and when to escalate.
Sample Checklist:
- Confirm the transaction is within the relevant authority limit.
- Verify the actual transaction was approved, not merely a supporting document.
- Confirm approval occurred before the transaction where required.
- Check that supporting evidence agrees with the transaction details.
- Record and escalate exceptions rather than forcing a “Yes” answer.
Official links:
- What Is Power Query? — Microsoft Learn
- Excel VBA reference — Microsoft Learn
2. Q: Why should staff understand the purpose of a control before using a checklist?
A: Show answer
A checklist becomes mechanical when users know what to tick but do not know why the control exists. Training should connect every major checklist item to a specific risk. An approval check may address unauthorized expenditure; a supporting-document check may address unsupported transactions; a coding check may address donor or project misclassification; and a reconciliation check may address incomplete or duplicated records. When staff understand the risk, they are more likely to investigate unusual evidence rather than accept the first document presented. This also makes the checklist more adaptable. If an unusual transaction does not fit the normal pattern, a knowledgeable user can identify the underlying control objective and decide what additional evidence is needed.

Send to the team:
- “Please learn the risk addressed by each control before completing the checklist.”
Key point:
- Controls are stronger when users understand their purpose.
- Risk awareness helps staff handle unusual transactions intelligently.
Sample Checklist:
- Identify the risk each checklist item is designed to address.
- Confirm staff can explain the reason for the control.
- Ask what could go wrong if the control failed.
- Identify acceptable evidence for the control.
- Confirm the escalation path for unresolved exceptions.
Official links:
- Use Power Query to transform data — Microsoft Learn
- Getting started with VBA in Office — Microsoft Learn
3. Q: How would you distinguish a checklist question from the evidence required to support it?
A: Show answer
The question and the evidence are different parts of a control. “Was procurement approved?” is a question. The evidence may include an approved request, procurement evaluation, delegated-authority record, or documented approval in the organization’s system. Training should prevent staff from treating any document containing a signature as sufficient evidence. The evidence must actually demonstrate the control occurred and must relate to the transaction under review. Where evidence is electronic, staff should also consider whether the system record is reliable and whether the approval can be traced to an authorized user. A checklist should therefore guide evidence evaluation rather than become a list of documents to collect.

Send to the team:
- “Please confirm that the evidence proves the control occurred for the transaction under review.”
Key point:
- A checklist question identifies what must be checked.
- Evidence must demonstrate that the control actually operated.
Sample Checklist:
- State the control question clearly.
- Identify the expected evidence type.
- Match the evidence to the transaction reference.
- Confirm the approver and authority level.
- Note any evidence gap or inconsistency.
Official links:
- Power Query documentation — Microsoft Learn
- Excel VBA reference — Microsoft Learn
4. Q: Why should training include examples where every checklist item appears complete but the transaction is still wrong?
A: Show answer
This is one of the most effective ways to teach judgment. A transaction could have an approval, invoice, payment voucher, budget code, and bank evidence but still be inappropriate because the expenditure is outside the donor rules, the supplier relationship creates a conflict of interest, the service was not actually received, or the amount was recorded in the wrong reporting period. These cases demonstrate that checklist completeness and accounting correctness are related but different concepts. Staff should learn to ask whether the transaction makes sense as a whole. The exercise should also teach them to challenge documentation that appears unusually perfect when operational evidence does not support it. The objective is not to make staff suspicious of everything but to teach them to reconcile the documents with the underlying event.

Send to the team:
- “Please consider whether the complete document set makes sense in substance, not only whether every document is present.”
Key point:
- Documentation can be complete while the transaction remains inappropriate.
- Training should connect documentation to the underlying economic event.
Sample Checklist:
- Are all required documents present?
- Do the documents describe the same transaction?
- Did the goods or services actually occur?
- Is the expenditure permitted under the applicable funding rules?
- Does the accounting treatment agree with the underlying event?
Official links:
- What Is Power Query? — Microsoft Learn
- Excel VBA reference — Microsoft Learn
5. Q: How would you teach staff to distinguish compliance with a procedure from effective control performance?
A: Show answer
Following a procedure means completing the prescribed steps. Effective control performance means those steps actually reduce the relevant risk. Staff therefore need to understand that “procedure completed” is not the final question. For example, a bank reconciliation may have been signed, but if old unmatched items were carried forward without investigation, the reconciliation may not provide effective control. Similarly, a budget review may have taken place, but if significant variances were not discussed or assigned to responsible managers, the review may have achieved little. Training should use examples where the procedure exists on paper but the control objective has not been achieved. This shifts attention from administrative completion to evidence of control effectiveness.

Send to the team:
- “Please assess whether the control reduced the risk, not only whether the procedure was completed.”
Key point:
- Procedure compliance and control effectiveness are not identical.
- Evidence of follow-up is important where a control identifies a problem.
Sample Checklist:
- Identify the risk addressed by the procedure.
- Confirm the procedure was performed correctly.
- Review exceptions generated by the control.
- Confirm exceptions were investigated or escalated.
- Assess whether the control outcome reduced the identified risk.
Official links:
- Use Power Query to transform data — Microsoft Learn
- Getting started with VBA in Office — Microsoft Learn
6. Q: How should supervisors train staff to challenge a weak or incomplete checklist response?
A: Show answer
Supervisors should ask follow-up questions rather than simply correcting the answer themselves. A response such as “Yes, approved” should prompt questions such as who approved it, under what authority, when the approval occurred, and where the evidence is stored. This approach reinforces the reasoning behind the checklist and develops staff capability. Supervisors should also distinguish genuine uncertainty from careless completion. A staff member who identifies an ambiguous case and escalates it has demonstrated useful control awareness, whereas a staff member who marks “Yes” without evidence has not. The training environment should therefore reward appropriate escalation and evidence-based challenge rather than speed of checklist completion.

Send to the team:
- “Please challenge unsupported checklist answers by asking what evidence supports the conclusion.”
Key point:
- Supervisors should coach reasoning rather than merely correct answers.
- Appropriate escalation is a positive control behavior.
Sample Checklist:
- Ask what evidence supports the response.
- Confirm the evidence is current and transaction-specific.
- Ask who performed and reviewed the control.
- Determine whether any uncertainty remains.
- Record escalation where the evidence is insufficient.

- Excel VBA reference — Microsoft Learn
- Power Query documentation — Microsoft Learn
7. Q: What should staff be taught about judgment and escalation when a checklist does not clearly answer a case?
A: Show answer
A checklist cannot anticipate every transaction. Staff should therefore know when to stop applying the standard rule and escalate for professional review. Examples include unusual donor restrictions, unclear ownership of an asset, suspected duplicate payments, related-party concerns, significant unsupported balances, or transactions close to a reporting cut-off. Training should provide a clear escalation matrix showing who reviews technical accounting questions, donor compliance questions, procurement matters, and suspected control breaches. The checklist can then include an “exception” option that directs the user to the appropriate reviewer instead of forcing a yes/no response. This prevents staff from inventing answers simply because the template does not contain a suitable category.

Send to the team:
- “Please use the exception and escalation route whenever the standard checklist does not adequately address the transaction.”
Key point:
- Checklists need an explicit exception path.
- Good controls tell users when not to rely on routine processing.
Sample Checklist:
- Does the transaction fit a standard category?
- Is any accounting or donor treatment uncertain?
- Is the amount or risk significant enough for management review?
- Has the correct specialist or supervisor been identified?
- Is the escalation documented before final posting?
