Table 5
Payroll Disbursement Controls
1. Q: How would you train staff to control payroll-related disbursements?
A: Show answer
Training should present payroll as a controlled end-to-end process rather than simply a monthly payment exercise. Staff should understand the sequence from the authorized employee record through approved changes, payroll preparation, review, payment authorization, bank release, and reconciliation to the payroll register and general ledger. Finance should be trained to verify that the payroll population is supported, changes are properly authorized, calculations are reasonable, deductions are accounted for, and the final payment amount agrees with the approved payroll. Human Resources should remain responsible for employment records and personnel decisions, while Finance focuses on financial verification, accounting, payment controls, and reconciliation. The training should also cover unusual transactions such as advances, bonuses where applicable, final settlements, retroactive adjustments, and corrections. Staff should learn what evidence to inspect, which exceptions require investigation, and when an issue should be escalated before payment is released. Because payroll contains sensitive employee information, training should also explain role-based access, confidentiality, secure handling of reports, and appropriate use of payroll files. The objective is not to make every finance employee an HR specialist, but to ensure that each person understands the part of the control chain for which they are responsible. These principles are consistent with internal-control guidance emphasizing authorization, segregation of duties, accurate recording, review, and access restrictions.

Study instruction:
- Learn the complete payroll control chain and identify which responsibility belongs to HR, Finance, management, and the payment approver.
Key point:
- Payroll control begins before calculation and ends only after payment and accounting reconciliation are complete.
- Staff should understand their own control responsibilities without duplicating HR's role.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
2. Q: Why should payroll be treated as an end-to-end control process rather than only a payment process?
A: Show answer
A payroll payment can be correct in arithmetic terms while still being wrong from a control perspective. For example, an employee may no longer be authorized, a salary change may not have been approved, a terminated employee may remain in the payroll file, or a payment may be sent to an unapproved bank account. An end-to-end approach links the source personnel information to the payroll calculation, payment file, bank transaction, and general-ledger posting. Training should therefore show staff how one payroll transaction moves through the organization and where independent checks should occur. This helps employees recognize that control failures often arise at the interfaces between departments rather than inside the payroll calculation itself. The process should include documented inputs, defined approvals, restricted access, review of exceptions, payment authorization, and reconciliation. A useful training exercise is to trace one ordinary employee payment and one exceptional payment from beginning to end and identify the evidence supporting every stage.

Study instruction:
- Trace a payroll transaction from employee authorization through general-ledger reconciliation and identify every control point.
Key point:
- Payroll accuracy depends on the entire process, not merely the calculation.
- Each stage should have an identifiable owner and evidence of review.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
3. Q: What should Finance verify about the authorized payroll population?
A: Show answer
Finance should not assume that the payroll register itself proves that every employee is valid. The payroll population should be reconciled to an authorized employee master record or another controlled HR source. Finance can verify that employees appearing on payroll are active or otherwise legitimately entitled to payment for the period, and should investigate additions, removals, unusual status changes, and duplicate records. The precise evidence will depend on the organization's systems and policies, but examples include approved personnel actions, employment documentation, termination records, approved leave or status information, and authorized payroll master-file changes. Finance does not need to recreate HR's personnel process; it needs enough independent evidence to establish that the financial payment population is based on authorized information. Training should teach staff to compare the current payroll population with the prior period and focus attention on changes rather than treating every employee record as a new transaction.

Study instruction:
- Practice reconciling the current payroll population to an authorized HR source and documenting unexplained additions or removals.
Key point:
- A payroll register should be based on an authorized employee population.
- Changes to the employee population deserve focused review.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual control activities
- U.S. OPM HR and Payroll Data Guidance
4. Q: How should staff control additions and removals from the payroll master file?
A: Show answer
Changes to the payroll master file should be treated as controlled transactions, not ordinary data entry. An addition should be supported by an authorized employment record and the required payroll information; a removal should be supported by an approved termination, separation, or other status change. The person entering the change should not automatically be the same person who approves it and releases the resulting payroll. The organization should also retain an audit trail showing what changed, when, by whom, and on whose authority. Periodic review of the master file is valuable because payroll fraud and errors can be created through seemingly small changes to employee status or bank information. Training should show staff how to compare the current and prior master files and investigate unexpected changes before the payroll run is finalized.

Study instruction:
- Learn to treat payroll master-file changes as independently reviewable events with documentary support.
Key point:
- Master-file amendments are high-value control points because they can alter future payments.
- Every material change should have authorization and traceable evidence.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual control activities
5. Q: How should approved salary changes be controlled before they reach payroll?
A: Show answer
Salary changes should originate from authorized personnel actions rather than informal instructions, emails, or verbal requests. Depending on the organization's policy, the evidence may include an approved salary adjustment, promotion, contract amendment, allowance decision, or other documented personnel action. Finance should verify that the amount, effective date, employee identity, and approving authority agree with the supporting documentation. The payroll preparer should not have unrestricted authority to create and approve salary changes. Training should emphasize the difference between entering an approved change and deciding whether the change should occur. The former may belong to an administrative payroll role; the latter normally belongs to an authorized manager or HR process. Where possible, the payroll system should preserve the effective date and historical value so that retroactive changes can be explained and reviewed.

Study instruction:
- Review several hypothetical salary changes and identify the minimum approval evidence needed before payroll processing.
Key point:
- Finance verifies authorized changes; it should not substitute for the organization's personnel approval process.
- Effective dates and supporting approvals are essential to reliable payroll calculations.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual control activities
- U.S. OPM Personnel Documentation
6. Q: What role should attendance, timesheets, or time information play in payroll controls?
A: Show answer
Where pay depends on attendance, working hours, overtime, shifts, days worked, or other time-based information, the underlying time record becomes an important input to payroll. Training should show staff how approved time information enters the payroll process, who is responsible for approving it, and how unusual entries are investigated. Finance should not blindly accept a time report simply because it exists; it should use defined review rules appropriate to the organization's payroll structure. Examples of useful checks include unusual overtime, duplicate hours, missing approvals, implausible working patterns, or significant changes from previous periods. Salaried employees may require a different control model from hourly or time-based employees, so the procedure should reflect actual employment arrangements. The purpose is to ensure that the payroll calculation is supported by the correct underlying evidence rather than to create unnecessary administrative work.

Study instruction:
- Map the relationship between approved time records and payroll calculations for each relevant category of employee.
Key point:
- Time-based payroll requires reliable, approved source information.
- Review effort should focus on unusual or unsupported time entries.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Management Resources
7. Q: Why should payroll preparation and payroll payment authorization be separated?
A: Show answer
Separation of duties reduces the opportunity for one person to create an unauthorized payroll change and then cause the resulting payment to be released without independent review. Payroll preparation involves compiling and calculating employee payments, while payment authorization involves approving the final amount for release through the bank or payment system. Where staffing permits, different individuals should perform these roles. In small NGOs where complete separation is not feasible, compensating controls become important, such as stronger management review, restricted system access, independent review of the final payroll register, and documented bank-approval procedures. Training should make the distinction clear: preparing a payment file is not the same as authorizing the use of organizational funds. The principle applies even when payroll is processed electronically because system permissions can otherwise recreate the same segregation-of-duties problem inside software.

Study instruction:
- Draw a payroll responsibility matrix showing who prepares, reviews, approves, releases, records, and reconciles payroll.
Key point:
- No single user should control every critical stage of payroll where effective segregation is practicable.
- Small teams need compensating controls when full separation is not possible.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
8. Q: How should a small NGO handle payroll segregation of duties when there are very few finance staff?
A: Show answer
A small NGO may not have enough employees to assign every payroll function to a different person, but that does not eliminate the control risk. The organization should identify incompatible duties and then design compensating controls around the staffing limitation. For example, one person may prepare the payroll, while a senior manager independently reviews the employee list, salary changes, payroll totals, and bank payment file before release. A separate person can review the bank reconciliation after payment. System access can also be restricted so that the preparer cannot both amend employee master records and approve the bank file. The organization should document the alternative control rather than treating the staffing limitation as an exception that needs no action. Training should teach staff that segregation is a control objective, not simply an organizational-chart requirement.

Study instruction:
- Build a compensating-control plan for a hypothetical NGO with only one payroll officer and one Finance Manager.
Key point:
- Limited staffing changes the control design but does not remove the need for independent review.
- Compensating controls should be explicit, documented, and consistently performed.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
9. Q: What should the payroll preparer check before calculating the monthly payroll?
A: Show answer
Before calculation, the preparer should confirm that the source files and master records are complete, current, and approved. The checklist may include the employee population, approved new hires, terminations, salary changes, allowances, deductions, approved leave or time information where applicable, prior-period unresolved items, and unusual one-off payments. The preparer should confirm that the correct payroll period and pay rates are being used and that no obsolete data source has accidentally been carried forward. A controlled checklist reduces reliance on memory, especially when the same payroll process is repeated under tight month-end deadlines. Training should emphasize that preparation is a control stage: the preparer should record the checks performed and identify exceptions before calculation rather than assuming the system will identify every problem automatically.

Study instruction:
- Create a pre-payroll checklist that identifies required inputs, approvals, and unresolved exceptions before calculation begins.
Key point:
- Payroll accuracy depends heavily on the quality and authorization of input data.
- A documented pre-processing checklist makes the process repeatable.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
10. Q: How would you teach staff to review the payroll register before payment?
A: Show answer
Staff should be trained to review the payroll register as a financial-control document, not merely as a list of employee names and amounts. The review should compare current-period totals with the prior period, investigate material changes, confirm that approved salary and personnel changes are reflected, review unusual allowances or deductions, and check whether the total payroll agrees with the payment proposal. The reviewer should also confirm that the payroll population is complete and that exceptions identified during preparation have been resolved or formally approved. A good review is risk-based: ordinary unchanged salaries generally require less investigation than new employees, large increases, unusual payments, or unexpected changes in deductions. The reviewer should document conclusions rather than merely initialing the register. Evidence of independent review is part of the control itself.

Study instruction:
- Practice performing a payroll-register review using prior-period comparison and an exception list.
Key point:
- The reviewer should investigate changes rather than simply confirm that the payroll total looks reasonable.
- Review evidence should show what was checked and how exceptions were resolved.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
11. Q: What payroll movements should receive additional review?
A: Show answer
Additional review should focus on movements that are unusual, material, unsupported, or inconsistent with expected personnel activity. Examples can include new employees, terminated employees still appearing on payroll, large salary increases, unusual overtime, one-off allowances, retroactive adjustments, significant changes in deductions, duplicate bank accounts, unexpected changes in net pay, or an unusual increase in total payroll cost. The exact thresholds should reflect organizational risk and payroll size rather than applying the same amount to every NGO. A practical method is to compare current payroll with the prior period and the approved staffing or salary plan, then use predefined rules to generate an exception list. Training should emphasize that an exception is not automatically an error; it is an item that requires an explanation and evidence before the reviewer is satisfied.

Study instruction:
- Develop an exception report containing at least five payroll tests and define what evidence would resolve each exception.
Key point:
- Exception reporting should direct attention toward changes that need explanation.
- An unusual item is a review signal, not proof of wrongdoing.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
12. Q: How should Finance reconcile payroll to the general ledger?
A: Show answer
The payroll register, payroll payment record, and general-ledger postings should be reconciled so that the financial records reflect the same approved payroll activity. The reconciliation should explain gross payroll, employee deductions, employer-related costs where applicable, net pay, payroll liabilities, and any other material payroll components according to the organization's accounting structure. Differences should be identified and explained rather than forced to agree by posting arbitrary adjustments. Training should show staff how to trace a difference from the general ledger back to the payroll register, payment file, or payroll journal. The reconciliation should be completed promptly after posting so errors do not remain hidden until the next payroll cycle. A good reconciliation also identifies stale payroll liabilities, reversed payments, corrections, or items that need follow-up with HR or the payroll provider.

Study instruction:
- Build a payroll-to-general-ledger reconciliation that explains every difference and shows the supporting evidence.
Key point:
- Payroll, cash payments, liabilities, and the general ledger should form one reconcilable chain.
- Unexplained differences should remain open until resolved.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
13. Q: How should payroll payments be reconciled to the bank after release?
A: Show answer
After the bank payment is released, Finance should confirm that the actual bank transaction agrees with the approved payroll payment file and that the amount was posted correctly in the accounting records. The reconciliation should identify failed or returned payments, duplicate payments, rejected transactions, unexpected bank charges, or differences between the payment file and the bank statement. Where the bank account contains several transactions, the payroll payment should be traceable through a unique reference or batch identifier. Training should reinforce that approval before payment and reconciliation after payment are separate controls. A person who approved the payroll should not assume that the bank processed it exactly as intended. The post-payment review provides an independent check that the cash leaving the organization corresponds to the authorized payroll.

Study instruction:
- Trace one payroll batch from the approved payment file to the bank statement and accounting entry.
Key point:
- Payment approval controls the intended transaction; bank reconciliation confirms the actual transaction.
- Failed, returned, or unusual payroll payments need documented follow-up.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
14. Q: Why should payroll deductions and liabilities be reconciled separately from net salary payments?
A: Show answer
Net salary is only one part of payroll accounting. Deductions and employer-related liabilities may create amounts that must later be remitted to tax authorities, pension administrators, insurers, benefit providers, or other parties depending on local rules and employment arrangements. Training should therefore distinguish between the employee's net pay, amounts withheld from employees, amounts borne by the employer, and the resulting liability balances. Each category should be reconciled from payroll reports to the accounting records and then to actual remittances where applicable. Differences may indicate a calculation problem, an unposted liability, a timing issue, or a remittance that has not been recorded correctly. Finance should not assume that because employees received the correct net amounts, the payroll accounting is complete.

Study instruction:
- Map each payroll component from calculation through liability recognition and eventual settlement.
Key point:
- Correct net pay does not prove that payroll liabilities are correct.
- Payroll deductions require their own reconciliation and settlement controls.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
15. Q: How should staff investigate an unexpected increase in total payroll cost?
A: Show answer
The first step should be to separate legitimate operational explanations from control errors. Finance should compare the current payroll with the prior period and the approved staffing or salary plan, then identify which employees or payroll components caused the movement. Possible explanations include new hires, salary changes, temporary allowances, overtime, exchange-rate effects, back-pay, annual adjustments, or changes in employer-related costs. The reviewer should request supporting evidence for material movements and confirm that the effective dates agree with approved personnel information. If the explanation cannot be supported, the item should remain an exception and be escalated before the financial result is considered final. Training should teach staff to investigate the composition of a variance rather than accepting a plausible verbal explanation without documentation.

Study instruction:
- Perform a payroll variance analysis and trace the largest movements to employee-level supporting evidence.
Key point:
- Payroll-cost increases should be explained by identifiable operational or approved accounting changes.
- Unsupported explanations should remain exceptions until evidence is obtained.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
16. Q: How should staff investigate an unexpected decrease in payroll cost?
A: Show answer
An unexplained decrease can be as important as an unexplained increase. Finance should determine whether the decrease resulted from approved departures, vacancies, unpaid leave, reduced time-based payments, changes in allowances, exchange rates, corrections, timing differences, or other legitimate causes. The review should compare employee counts and key payroll components rather than looking only at the total amount. A large decrease might indicate that a payment is missing, a staff member was omitted, a salary change was incorrectly applied, or a liability was posted differently. Training should teach staff to evaluate whether the decrease is operationally consistent with what management expected for the period. The objective is not to prevent every variance but to ensure that material variances are understood and supported.

Study instruction:
- Review a hypothetical payroll decrease and identify the evidence needed to distinguish legitimate savings from an omitted or misclassified payment.
Key point:
- Payroll control requires investigation of both increases and decreases.
- Variance analysis should connect financial movements to personnel changes and operational reality.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
17. Q: How should Finance distinguish routine payroll from exceptional payments?
A: Show answer
Routine payroll normally follows a repeatable monthly process, while exceptional payments may require additional evidence and approval. Examples include salary advances, final settlements, back-pay, corrections, one-time allowances, bonuses where applicable, or payments made after an employee leaves. These transactions should not simply be added to routine payroll without identifying their basis and approval. Training should show staff how to maintain a separate exception list and how to verify the specific authority and supporting evidence for each unusual item. Exceptional transactions may also require special accounting or disclosure treatment depending on the circumstances. The important control is that staff recognize that unusual transactions deserve a different level of scrutiny even when the payroll system processes them in the same batch as ordinary salary payments.

Study instruction:
- Create a separate control schedule for exceptional payroll transactions and identify the evidence required for each type.
Key point:
- One-off payroll transactions require focused review because they fall outside the normal recurring pattern.
- Routine processing should not make exceptional items invisible.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual control activities
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
18. Q: How should salary advances be controlled in payroll?
A: Show answer
A salary advance should be supported by an approved request, an identifiable employee, the amount authorized, repayment or deduction arrangements, and evidence of how the advance will be cleared. Finance should not allow an advance to disappear into the regular payroll without maintaining a separate record of the outstanding balance. When payroll deductions are used for recovery, the payroll register should show the deduction and the advance schedule should be reduced accordingly. The control schedule should identify opening balance, new advances, deductions, adjustments, and closing balance. Training should teach staff to distinguish the advance from salary expense: the financial treatment depends on the organization's accounting policy and the nature of the advance, but in many situations the outstanding amount represents a recoverable balance until settled.

Study instruction:
- Practice reconciling an employee advance schedule to payroll deductions and outstanding balances.
Key point:
- An advance should remain traceable until it is fully settled.
- Payroll deductions should be matched back to the advance record.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
19. Q: How should final salary settlements for departing employees be controlled?
A: Show answer
Final settlements should be treated as controlled exceptions because they may combine salary through the last working date, approved leave balances where applicable, deductions, recoveries, benefits, and other contractual or policy-based amounts. HR should confirm the employment status and relevant personnel information, while Finance should verify the calculation and accounting treatment against authorized documents. The settlement should be reviewed independently before payment, especially where several components are combined. Training should show staff how to identify the employee's final eligible period, reconcile deductions and recoveries, and ensure that the employee is removed or appropriately updated in future payroll runs. The final settlement should also be connected to the organization's clearance process where applicable so that outstanding advances, assets, or other recoverable balances are not overlooked.

Study instruction:
- Work through a sample final settlement and identify every component requiring evidence or independent review.
Key point:
- Final settlements combine several control areas and should receive focused review.
- Departure controls should prevent both overpayment and failure to recover valid balances.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual control activities
- U.S. OPM Personnel Documentation
20. Q: Why should payroll payment files be independently reviewed before submission to the bank?
A: Show answer
The final payment file is the point at which payroll information becomes an instruction to move organizational cash. A reviewer should therefore compare the payment file with the approved payroll register and investigate differences before submission. The review can include total amount, number of payments, bank-account changes, unusual beneficiaries, duplicate entries, and the payment batch reference. The reviewer should have enough information to establish that the file corresponds to the approved payroll and should document approval in accordance with the organization's banking process. The person who creates the file should not automatically be the sole person who releases it. Training should make this distinction explicit because electronic banking can make a payment appear routine even though the control consequences are significant.

Study instruction:
- Use a sample payroll payment file to identify checks that should occur before bank release.
Key point:
- The payment file is a direct instruction to move cash and requires independent authorization.
- File review should reconcile the bank instruction to the approved payroll.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
21. Q: How should bank-account changes for employees be controlled?
A: Show answer
Changes to an employee's bank account or payment destination should be treated as sensitive master-data changes. The change should have a clear request, verification through an approved channel, and independent review before it is used for payroll. Organizations should avoid relying solely on an email address or unverified instruction where the risk of impersonation is significant. The system should preserve who made the change and when it became effective. A useful payroll review is to produce a list of employees whose payment details changed during the period and require evidence for each change. Training should emphasize that a valid employee record does not automatically make every associated bank account valid. The payment destination is part of the disbursement control and should be protected accordingly.

Study instruction:
- Design a bank-account-change workflow with request, verification, approval, data entry, and independent review stages.
Key point:
- Employee payment details are sensitive master data that can redirect cash.
- Changes should be independently verified and traceable.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual control activities
- NIST Cybersecurity Framework Payroll Profile
22. Q: What access controls should apply to payroll information?
A: Show answer
Access should be limited to people whose roles require payroll information or the ability to perform specific payroll functions. Not every Finance employee needs access to full employee-level salary or personal information, and operational access should be separated from approval authority where practicable. Permissions should reflect job responsibilities, and accounts should be removed or changed promptly when employees leave or change roles. Sensitive payroll files should be stored and transmitted through approved organizational systems rather than informal personal channels. Training should cover both system permissions and practical behavior, such as not leaving payroll reports visible on shared desks or sending unprotected employee information to unintended recipients. The appropriate level of protection depends on the NGO's legal, contractual, donor, and information-security obligations, but the underlying control principle is to restrict access according to legitimate business need.

Study instruction:
- Review a payroll access matrix and identify users who have more access than their duties require.
Key point:
- Payroll information should be accessible according to role and legitimate business need.
- Access rights should be reviewed when responsibilities change.
Source links:
- NIST Cybersecurity Framework Payroll Profile
- NIST SP 800-171 Access Control
23. Q: Why should payroll access be reviewed when staff join, leave, or change roles?
A: Show answer
Payroll access should follow the employee lifecycle rather than remain permanently attached to a user account. New users should receive only the permissions needed for their duties. Employees transferring to other roles may need some permissions removed and others added. Departing employees should lose access promptly according to the organization's procedures. Periodic access reviews are also valuable because permissions can accumulate when changes are made without removing old rights. Training should teach managers and system administrators to view access as a controlled resource rather than a one-time IT setup task. An audit trail should help establish who had access and, where the system allows it, what important payroll actions were performed.

Study instruction:
- Develop an onboarding, role-change, and offboarding checklist specifically for payroll-system access.
Key point:
- Payroll access should change with job responsibilities and employment status.
- Periodic review helps identify accumulated or unnecessary permissions.
Source links:
- NIST SP 800-171 Access Control
- NIST Cybersecurity Framework Payroll Profile
24. Q: How should payroll confidentiality be incorporated into staff training?
A: Show answer
Confidentiality should be presented as a practical control responsibility, not merely as a general instruction to "keep information private." Staff should understand what payroll information is sensitive, who is authorized to receive it, how reports should be stored, how files should be transferred, and what to do if information is sent to the wrong person. Training should also distinguish between legitimate management reporting and unnecessary exposure of employee-level details. A management dashboard may need aggregate payroll information while not requiring individual salaries. This principle supports data minimization: staff should use and disclose only the information needed for the legitimate task. The organization should also define how payroll records are retained and destroyed under applicable requirements.

Study instruction:
- Create examples showing when aggregate payroll reporting is sufficient and when employee-level information is legitimately required.
Key point:
- Payroll confidentiality depends on limiting access, use, disclosure, and retention to legitimate needs.
- Staff need practical rules for handling sensitive data in daily work.
Source links:
- NIST Cybersecurity Framework Payroll Profile
- NIST SP 800-53 Privacy and Security Controls
25. Q: How would you train staff to identify duplicate payroll payments?
A: Show answer
Duplicate-payment review should combine automated tests with human investigation. A basic test can compare employee identifiers, payment dates, net amounts, bank details, and payment references to identify potential duplicates. However, legitimate situations can also produce similar amounts, so the system should flag possible duplicates rather than automatically reversing them. Training should show staff how to investigate whether a duplicate is genuine, determine whether the bank actually processed both payments, identify the cause, and record the recovery or correction. Where technology permits, system controls can prevent exact duplicate records from being entered, but manual review remains important for near-duplicates created by corrections or reprocessing. The exception report should distinguish between a suspected duplicate and a confirmed duplicate so management does not confuse a control alert with a proven error.

Study instruction:
- Build a duplicate-payments review using multiple fields rather than relying only on the net amount.
Key point:
- Duplicate detection should combine automated matching with documented human review.
- A flagged transaction is an exception requiring investigation, not an automatic conclusion.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
26. Q: What common payroll-control mistakes should staff be trained to avoid?
A: Show answer
Common weaknesses include allowing one person to prepare and approve payroll without independent review, processing salary changes without documented authorization, leaving former employees on the payroll, failing to review unusual movements, relying on verbal approval, using shared user accounts, allowing excessive access to payroll data, failing to reconcile payroll to the general ledger, and treating bank confirmation as sufficient proof that the payroll was correct. Another common mistake is focusing entirely on the total payroll amount while ignoring changes in the underlying employee population. Training should use practical examples rather than simply listing risks. Staff should be asked to identify what evidence is missing, which control failed, and what corrective action should occur. The objective is to build a habit of asking whether a payroll transaction is authorized, supported, independently reviewed, and accurately recorded.

Study instruction:
- Review sample payroll errors and classify each as an authorization, access, processing, review, reconciliation, or documentation weakness.
Key point:
- Many payroll failures arose from weak process controls rather than from arithmetic errors.
- Training should connect each common mistake to a specific preventive or detective control.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
27. Q: How should staff respond if a payroll change is requested after the normal cut-off date?
A: Show answer
Late changes should enter an exception process rather than bypassing the established control cycle. The request should identify the employee, the change, the effective date, the reason for urgency, the approving authority, and the financial effect. The organization should determine whether the change can appropriately be included in the current payroll or should be processed in the next cycle or through a separate approved correction. Training should emphasize that urgency does not automatically eliminate the need for evidence or authorization. If a manual override is necessary, the override should be documented and reviewed after processing. A recurring pattern of late payroll changes may also indicate an underlying HR or process problem that management should address rather than treating every late request as an isolated exception.

Study instruction:
- Create an exception form for late payroll changes and define the minimum approval and evidence requirements.
Key point:
- A deadline does not replace authorization or supporting evidence.
- Repeated late changes should be analyzed as a process weakness.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual control activities
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
28. Q: What would you do if HR and Finance disagree about a payroll amount?
A: Show answer
The issue should be resolved through evidence and clearly defined responsibilities rather than informal negotiation over the final amount. HR should provide the approved personnel basis for the payment, while Finance should verify the financial calculation, accounting treatment, and payment impact. If the disagreement concerns policy interpretation, the matter should be escalated to the designated authority rather than settled by whichever person is more senior in the moment. The underlying documents should be retained so the final decision is traceable. Training should teach staff to separate three questions: what employment arrangement was approved, how that arrangement should be translated into payroll, and how the resulting transaction should be accounted for and paid. This prevents Finance from making personnel decisions or HR from bypassing financial controls.

Study instruction:
- Work through a payroll disagreement case and identify which question belongs to HR, which belongs to Finance, and which requires management escalation.
Key point:
- Payroll disputes should be resolved by evidence, role clarity, and documented authority.
- Finance and HR have complementary responsibilities rather than identical responsibilities.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. OPM Personnel Documentation
29. Q: How should payroll corrections be controlled after a payroll run has already been approved?
A: Show answer
Post-approval corrections should be treated as controlled amendments rather than informal edits to a finalized payroll. The organization should identify what was wrong, why the correction is necessary, the affected employee or employees, the amount, and the approving authority. The correction should be distinguishable from the original payroll so that the audit trail shows both the initial transaction and the subsequent change. Where a payment has already been made, Finance should determine whether a recovery, supplemental payment, or accounting adjustment is required. Training should discourage users from silently changing the payroll file after approval because such edits can make it impossible to determine what was originally reviewed. A controlled correction process preserves the integrity of the approved payroll while allowing genuine errors to be fixed.

Study instruction:
- Design a correction log that captures the original payroll reference, error, correction, approval, payment effect, and accounting effect.
Key point:
- Corrections should add to the audit trail rather than overwrite the original transaction history.
- Post-approval changes require documented authorization and review.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual control activities
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
30. Q: How can payroll automation improve control without replacing human review?
A: Show answer
Automation can perform repeatable tests such as comparing employee populations, identifying unusual salary changes, detecting duplicate payments, checking missing fields, reconciling totals, and producing exception reports. It can also reduce manual re-entry between payroll, payment, and accounting systems. However, automation should not be treated as proof that a transaction is correct. A system may correctly process inaccurate or unauthorized data if the source records are wrong. Training should therefore teach staff to divide the process into automated routine checks and human judgment points. The system should produce transparent exception lists and logs rather than simply generating a final number. Human reviewers remain responsible for deciding whether an exception is legitimate, whether evidence is sufficient, and whether escalation is required.

Study instruction:
- Identify which payroll checks can be automated safely and which decisions must remain subject to human approval.
Key point:
- Automation strengthens repeatable controls but does not eliminate the need for independent review.
- The strongest design combines automated detection with documented human judgment.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
31. Q: How would you use an exception report to train payroll reviewers?
A: Show answer
An exception report should convert large volumes of payroll data into a manageable review list. Training can demonstrate tests for new employees, terminated employees, large pay changes, duplicate payment indicators, unusual deductions, bank-account changes, missing approvals, and significant period-over-period movements. Each exception should have a status such as open, under review, resolved, or escalated, along with the reviewer, date, evidence, and conclusion. The system should not hide legitimate exceptions merely to produce a clean report. Instead, management should understand that a good control report makes uncertainty visible so that people can investigate it. Over time, recurring exceptions can be analyzed to determine whether the root cause lies in HR processes, payroll configuration, data quality, or management approvals.

Study instruction:
- Design an exception register with fields for issue, owner, evidence, conclusion, corrective action, and closure date.
Key point:
- Exception reports focus human effort on transactions that require judgment.
- Recurring exceptions can reveal weaknesses in the underlying process.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
32. Q: How should managers know whether payroll controls are actually working?
A: Show answer
Management should monitor indicators that demonstrate both compliance with the process and the quality of outcomes. Useful measures can include the percentage of payrolls completed on time, unresolved payroll exceptions, number and value of corrections after approval, failed or returned payments, unreconciled payroll balances, unauthorized-access incidents, overdue access reviews, and recurring master-file errors. The measures should not become a target that encourages staff to suppress exceptions. For example, a low exception count is not automatically evidence of strong controls if the testing itself is weak. Managers should review trends, root causes, and whether corrective actions were completed. Training should explain the difference between measuring activity, such as completing a checklist, and measuring control effectiveness, such as whether identified errors were detected and corrected before payment.

Study instruction:
- Build a payroll-control dashboard containing both process-completion measures and substantive exception measures.
Key point:
- Effective monitoring evaluates whether controls detect and prevent problems, not merely whether forms were completed.
- Control metrics should be interpreted in context rather than treated as isolated performance targets.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
33. Q: How should payroll procedures be documented so that another staff member can take over the process?
A: Show answer
The payroll procedure should document the monthly timetable, source reports, responsible roles, approval points, key checks, exception handling, reconciliation requirements, and evidence-retention expectations. It should explain where the employee population comes from, how approved changes are received, who prepares the payroll, who reviews it, who authorizes payment, and how the accounting and bank reconciliations are completed. The procedure should also explain what to do when a normal step fails, such as a missing approval, rejected bank payment, or payroll-system error. A checklist can accompany the detailed procedure so staff have a practical tool for each payroll cycle. Documentation should be maintained with version control so staff know which procedure is current.

Study instruction:
- Create a payroll procedure manual and a one-page monthly checklist that reflect the same control sequence.
Key point:
- Good documentation makes the process repeatable and reduces dependence on one experienced individual.
- Procedures should cover exceptions as well as the normal monthly cycle.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
34. Q: What evidence should be retained to support a completed payroll cycle?
A: Show answer
The evidence set should be sufficient to demonstrate that payroll was authorized, correctly processed, independently reviewed, paid, and reconciled. Depending on the organization's process, this may include the approved payroll register, employee master-file changes, salary or personnel approvals, time records where applicable, exception reports, payment-approval evidence, bank confirmation, payroll-to-ledger reconciliation, and evidence of resolution for significant exceptions. The exact retention period should follow applicable law, contractual requirements, donor requirements, and organizational policy. Training should emphasize that evidence should be understandable to someone who was not present when the payroll was processed. A reviewer should be able to reconstruct the key decisions from the retained records without relying on informal explanations or staff memory.

Study instruction:
- Assemble a model payroll audit file and test whether an independent reviewer could understand the complete payroll cycle from the retained evidence.
Key point:
- Evidence should demonstrate authorization, review, payment, recording, and reconciliation.
- Records should be organized so that an independent reviewer can reconstruct what happened.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
35. Q: How would you train staff to respond to a suspected unauthorized payroll payment?
A: Show answer
The response should prioritize containment, evidence preservation, confirmation of the facts, and appropriate escalation. Staff should know who must be notified when a payment appears unauthorized and should avoid altering records in a way that destroys the audit trail. Finance should establish the amount, employee or beneficiary involved, payment date, authorization trail, bank status, and relevant master-file changes. HR, management, banking contacts, internal audit, legal advisers, or other functions should be involved according to the organization's incident procedures and applicable obligations. The immediate objective is to prevent further loss or repetition while preserving accurate records. After the immediate issue is controlled, management should determine the root cause and whether the process needs stronger access controls, approvals, monitoring, or staff training.

Study instruction:
- Work through a suspected unauthorized-payment scenario and identify the first five control actions that should occur.
Key point:
- Suspected unauthorized payments require prompt escalation and preservation of evidence.
- Corrective action should address both the transaction and the underlying control weakness.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
- NIST Cybersecurity Framework Payroll Profile
36. Q: How should payroll training distinguish prevention from detection and correction?
A: Show answer
Payroll controls work at different stages. Preventive controls are designed to stop inappropriate transactions before they occur, such as authorization requirements, role-based access, segregation of duties, and controlled master-file changes. Detective controls identify problems after or during processing, such as payroll variance analysis, duplicate-payment reports, exception reviews, and reconciliations. Corrective controls address identified problems through approved corrections, recovery of overpayments, reversal or reprocessing, and root-cause remediation. Staff should understand that no single control is expected to achieve everything. Training should show how the three categories work together across the payroll cycle. For example, restricted access may prevent an unauthorized change, while a change report can detect one that occurred, and a correction process can address the consequences if it was processed.

Study instruction:
- Classify each payroll control in a sample process as preventive, detective, corrective, or a combination.
Key point:
- Strong payroll control design uses multiple layers rather than relying on one check.
- Prevention, detection, and correction serve different purposes in the same control framework.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
37. Q: How should finance staff communicate payroll issues to senior management?
A: Show answer
Payroll reporting to management should focus on material issues, decisions required, and control implications rather than overwhelming managers with employee-level details. A useful monthly summary can report total payroll against budget or forecast, major movements, exceptional payments, unresolved reconciliations, significant control exceptions, and actions requiring management attention. Sensitive employee information should be restricted to those who need it for the decision being made. When an issue is serious, the report should identify what happened, the financial effect, whether payment has occurred, what evidence has been confirmed, what immediate action has been taken, and what decision or escalation is required. Training should help Finance separate confidential operational detail from management-level information so that control issues are communicated without unnecessary disclosure.

Study instruction:
- Draft a management payroll summary using aggregate information and a separate confidential exception schedule for authorized reviewers.
Key point:
- Management reporting should be decision-oriented while protecting employee confidentiality.
- Significant payroll issues should be communicated with facts, financial impact, action taken, and next steps.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- NIST Cybersecurity Framework Payroll Profile
38. Q: How should payroll controls be tested periodically rather than assumed to be effective?
A: Show answer
A payroll control can exist on paper and still fail in practice. Periodic testing should examine whether the control was actually performed, by the correct person, using appropriate evidence, and with exceptions properly resolved. Tests can include samples of salary changes, employee additions and removals, payment approvals, bank-account changes, reconciliations, access reviews, and correction entries. Management should also investigate recurring exceptions because repeated findings may show that the control design or implementation needs improvement. Testing should be risk-based and proportionate to the size and complexity of the payroll process. Training should help staff distinguish between verifying that a checklist exists and verifying that the underlying control objective was achieved.

Study instruction:
- Design a quarterly payroll-control testing program covering authorization, access, processing, payment, and reconciliation controls.
Key point:
- A documented procedure is not evidence that the control worked.
- Periodic testing should examine performance, evidence, exceptions, and corrective action.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual, Volume 1
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
39. Q: How can NGOs use a payroll control matrix to clarify staff responsibilities?
A: Show answer
A control matrix can list each major payroll activity, the risk it addresses, the control performed, the responsible person, the required evidence, the frequency, and the independent reviewer. For example, the matrix can separate HR authorization of personnel changes, payroll preparation by Finance or a payroll officer, management review of the payroll register, bank approval of the payment file, and Finance reconciliation to the ledger and bank. It can also identify compensating controls where staffing is limited. This approach helps prevent the common problem where everyone assumes someone else is checking a key stage. Training should use the matrix as a practical map of accountability rather than as a document that exists only for auditors.

Study instruction:
- Build a payroll control matrix and verify that every critical activity has an owner, evidence requirement, and review mechanism.
Key point:
- A control matrix makes ownership visible and exposes gaps or excessive concentration of duties.
- Responsibilities should be defined before payroll problems occur.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Standards for Internal Control
- U.S. GAO Internal Control Management and Evaluation Tool
40. Q: What would a complete monthly payroll training checklist contain?
A: Show answer
A practical training checklist should follow the payroll cycle in order. It should cover confirmation of the authorized employee population; review and approval of additions, removals, and salary changes; validation of time or attendance inputs where applicable; preparation of the payroll; review of unusual movements; review of deductions and liabilities; independent approval of the payroll register; independent review and authorization of the bank payment file; confirmation of actual bank processing; posting and reconciliation to the general ledger; review of outstanding exceptions; and secure filing of supporting evidence. The checklist should also include access-control considerations, because payroll data must remain restricted throughout the process. New staff should be trained using real or carefully controlled examples and should be required to demonstrate that they can identify an exception, locate the supporting evidence, and explain the escalation route. Periodic refresher training should focus on control failures and recurring exceptions rather than repeating only the basic procedure.

Study instruction:
- Use the checklist to simulate a complete month-end payroll cycle and document the evidence produced at each stage.
Key point:
- Training should connect people, evidence, approvals, payments, accounting, reconciliation, and confidentiality into one controlled process.
- The best training prepares staff to identify and respond to exceptions, not merely complete routine steps.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO Financial Audit Manual
- NIST Cybersecurity Framework Payroll Profile
