Table 6
Procurement Controls Before Disbursement
1. Q: How would you train staff to apply procurement requirements before disbursement?
A: Show answer
Finance staff do not necessarily own procurement decisions, but they need to understand the procurement controls that affect payment eligibility. Training should explain the relationship between requisition, quotation or tender requirements where applicable, purchase order or contract, vendor selection, delivery evidence, invoice, approval, and payment. Finance should know what evidence it is expected to verify and what exceptions require escalation to procurement or management. Staff should avoid approving a payment simply because an invoice exists. Instead, they should confirm that the underlying purchasing process has reached the stage required by the organization’s policy and that any exceptions are formally documented. The exact threshold, competition method, approval level, and evidence required should always come from the applicable procurement policy, donor agreement, grant terms, and local requirements.

Study instruction:
- Understand the full procure-to-pay chain and the specific point at which Finance is responsible for checking payment support.

Key point:
- An invoice alone is not sufficient evidence that procurement controls were satisfied.
- Finance should verify required evidence and escalate documented exceptions.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
2. Q: Why should Finance understand procurement even when Procurement owns the purchasing process?
A: Show answer
Finance becomes a control point when an approved purchasing decision reaches the disbursement stage. It needs enough procurement knowledge to determine whether the payment package is complete, whether the transaction has reached the required approval stage, and whether obvious control failures need escalation. This does not mean Finance should redo procurement or substitute its judgment for the procurement function. Instead, roles should be clearly separated. Procurement confirms sourcing and purchasing compliance; the responsible manager confirms need and receipt; Finance confirms financial coding, authorization, supporting evidence, and payment eligibility under the applicable policy. Clear role separation reduces gaps in responsibility and helps prevent a situation where each function assumes another team performed the critical check.

Study instruction:
- Review the organization’s segregation of duties and map each procurement control to the function responsible for performing it.

Key point:
- Finance needs procurement awareness without taking over Procurement’s role.
- Clear control ownership prevents gaps and duplicated work.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- UNDP: Segregation of Duties
3. Q: What should staff understand about the purpose of pre-disbursement procurement checks?
A: Show answer
The purpose is to establish that the organization is paying for a legitimate, authorized, properly procured transaction rather than merely processing a valid-looking invoice. A pre-disbursement review helps confirm that the purchase was authorized, the procurement method was appropriate for the transaction, the selected supplier was supported by the required evaluation or justification, the goods or services were actually received, and the payment has the required approvals. It also provides an opportunity to identify donor-specific restrictions or exceptions before cash leaves the organization. Staff should understand that the control is preventative: it is designed to stop a weak transaction before payment rather than discover the problem during an audit after funds have already been disbursed.

Study instruction:
- Learn to distinguish preventive payment controls from post-payment audit checks.

Key point:
- Pre-disbursement controls prevent avoidable errors before cash leaves the organization.
- The review should cover both procurement compliance and evidence of receipt and approval.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
4. Q: What procurement documents should Finance expect to see before approving a payment?
A: Show answer
The exact document set depends on the transaction type and the organization’s policy, but Finance will commonly encounter a purchase requisition or request, evidence of the approved procurement method, quotations or tender documentation where required, an evaluation or selection record, approval of the supplier, purchase order or contract, delivery or service completion evidence, supplier invoice, and payment authorization. Some transactions also require tax documentation, conflict-of-interest declarations, donor approvals, inspection certificates, or evidence of competitive exceptions. Finance should use a transaction-specific checklist rather than assuming every purchase requires an identical package. The checklist should identify mandatory documents, conditional documents, and escalation triggers so staff can distinguish a genuinely complete payment file from one that merely contains an invoice and approval signature.

Study instruction:
- Build a procurement-payment checklist linked to transaction type, value, method, donor, and required approvals.

Key point:
- Required evidence should be defined before payment processing begins.
- Not every transaction needs the same documents, but every transaction needs a defensible evidence trail.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- UNDP Procurement at UNDP
5. Q: Why should staff check the requisition before reviewing the invoice?
A: Show answer
The requisition establishes the original business need and provides the starting point for the purchasing process. Reviewing it first helps Finance understand what was requested, which project or budget is expected to bear the cost, whether the request was authorized, and whether the later invoice relates to the original requirement. If Finance starts with only the invoice, it may miss that the purchase was never properly requested or that the final transaction materially differs from what was approved. The requisition is not proof that the supplier delivered the item, but it is an important part of the authorization chain. Training should therefore teach staff to follow the transaction from need through procurement to payment rather than treating each document as an isolated piece of paper.

Study instruction:
- Practice tracing sample transactions from the approved request through to final payment evidence.

Key point:
- The requisition establishes the approved business need.
- Finance should trace the transaction rather than review documents in isolation.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
6. Q: How should staff determine whether quotations or competitive bids were required?
A: Show answer
Staff should not rely on memory or informal thresholds. They should consult the current procurement policy, donor-specific requirements, delegation matrix, and any applicable local rules. The required method may depend on estimated value, category, risk, urgency, location, supplier market, or donor conditions. Training should teach staff to identify the applicable threshold before the purchase is made, because trying to reconstruct competition after a supplier has already been selected is a much weaker control. Where competition is required, Finance should look for evidence that the process was actually performed and documented. Where competition is not required, staff should still verify that the transaction followed the simplified method allowed by policy rather than assuming that “low value” means “no controls.”

Study instruction:
- Memorize where to find the current threshold matrix rather than relying on remembered amounts.

Key point:
- Procurement thresholds are policy-specific and should be checked before purchase.
- Simplified procurement still requires compliance with the relevant rule.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement at UNDP
- World Bank Procurement Framework
7. Q: Why should Finance verify the procurement method rather than simply count quotations?
A: Show answer
Counting quotations does not by itself prove that the correct procurement method was followed. A transaction may require a specific competitive process, a documented evaluation, a framework agreement, a direct-selection justification, or another method. Three quotations gathered informally may not satisfy a policy that requires formal solicitation or documented evaluation criteria. Finance should therefore verify both the method and the evidence supporting the method. Training should focus on the logic: first identify the applicable rule, then check whether the procurement file demonstrates compliance with that rule. This is more reliable than using a simplistic “three quotes equals compliant” test, which can fail when policy requirements are more specific or when the procurement is subject to donor-specific conditions.

Study instruction:
- Compare the organization’s procurement methods and document the minimum evidence expected for each one.

Key point:
- Compliance is about following the applicable method, not merely collecting a fixed number of quotations.
- The same number of quotes can mean different things under different policies.

Source links:
- World Bank: Evaluating Bids and Proposals
- UNDP Procurement at UNDP
8. Q: How should staff review vendor-selection evidence before payment?
A: Show answer
Staff should confirm that the selected vendor can be linked to the procurement process and that the reason for selection is documented in a way consistent with the applicable rules. Depending on the method, this may include quotations, bid submissions, an evaluation matrix, approval by a procurement committee, a framework agreement, or a written justification for direct procurement. Finance does not normally need to re-evaluate the technical merits of every bid, but it should be alert to obvious gaps such as an invoice from a vendor that was not the approved supplier, missing award documentation, unexplained changes after selection, or an unsupported sole-source decision. Such issues should be escalated before payment rather than silently corrected in the accounting file.

Study instruction:
- Learn which procurement documents demonstrate supplier selection for each purchasing method used by the organization.

Key point:
- The vendor on the invoice should be traceable to the documented procurement decision.
- Material gaps in selection evidence should be escalated before payment.

Source links:
- World Bank: Evaluating Bids and Proposals
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
9. Q: Why should Finance check the purchase order or contract before paying an invoice?
A: Show answer
The purchase order or contract translates the procurement decision into an enforceable commercial arrangement. It normally identifies the supplier, goods or services, quantities or scope, prices, payment terms, delivery requirements, and relevant conditions. Comparing the invoice to that approved commitment helps Finance determine whether the amount being requested is consistent with what the organization agreed to buy. Without this step, Finance may pay an invoice that exceeds the approved amount, covers an unapproved variation, or relates to goods or services that were never contracted. Where a contract has been amended, the amendment should also be reviewed so Finance is paying against the current authorized terms rather than an outdated version.

Study instruction:
- Practice comparing invoice details against the current purchase order or signed contract.

Key point:
- The contract or PO provides the financial baseline for the payment.
- Variations should be authorized and documented before they are paid.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
10. Q: How should Finance verify that goods or services were actually received?
A: Show answer
A payment should normally be supported by evidence that the organization received what it agreed to purchase. For goods this may be a delivery note, goods-received record, inspection report, store receipt, asset handover, or other approved evidence. For services it may be a signed completion certificate, timesheet, deliverable, activity report, acceptance note, or manager confirmation, depending on the contract. Finance should check that the evidence relates to the invoice and reflects the required quantity, period, or scope. A supplier invoice by itself proves that the supplier requested payment; it does not prove that the organization accepted the goods or services. Training should therefore emphasize evidence of receipt as a distinct control from supplier billing.

Study instruction:
- Build examples showing the difference between supplier billing evidence and evidence of organizational acceptance.

Key point:
- Payment should be supported by evidence of receipt or accepted performance.
- Different purchases require different forms of completion evidence.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
11. Q: What is the purpose of three-way matching in procurement payments?
A: Show answer
Three-way matching compares the purchase order, evidence of receipt, and supplier invoice before payment. The goal is to confirm that the organization ordered the item or service, actually received it, and is being billed for the agreed amount. The exact design can vary, especially for consulting, grants, milestone contracts, or other arrangements where a traditional goods receipt is not appropriate. Even then, the underlying principle remains useful: payment should connect an authorized commitment with evidence of performance and a valid claim for payment. Staff should understand that matching is not a mechanical exercise. A mismatch may be a legitimate timing issue, partial delivery, approved variation, or data error, and the correct response is to investigate and document the reason rather than simply override the control.

Study instruction:
- Practice identifying legitimate and illegitimate mismatches in sample payment packages.

Key point:
- Three-way matching links commitment, receipt, and billing.
- Exceptions should be investigated and documented rather than automatically overridden.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
12. Q: How should Finance handle a payment where the invoice is correct but procurement evidence is incomplete?
A: Show answer
The invoice should not automatically be paid merely because the amount appears reasonable. Finance should identify which procurement requirement is missing, determine whether the gap is a document-retrieval problem or a genuine process exception, and send the case to the responsible function for resolution. If the policy permits a formal exception, the required approval and justification should be documented before payment. If no exception is permitted, the transaction should be corrected or held according to policy. Training should teach staff to state the missing control precisely—for example, “no evidence of required competitive process” is more useful than “documents incomplete.” Precise exception descriptions make escalation faster and create a clear audit trail.

Study instruction:
- Learn to describe exceptions by control requirement, missing evidence, owner, and required corrective action.

Key point:
- A complete invoice does not cure an incomplete procurement process.
- Exceptions should be resolved through the approved escalation route.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
13. Q: How should staff distinguish a missing document from a procurement exception?
A: Show answer
A missing document means the control may have been performed but the evidence is not currently in the payment file. A procurement exception means the required control may not have been performed, was performed differently, or was intentionally bypassed under an approved exception process. The distinction matters because the response is different. Missing documentation may be resolved by retrieving the existing evidence; an actual exception may require a formal justification, management decision, corrective action, donor notification, or other treatment. Finance staff should not assume that because a procurement officer says the process occurred, the documentation is optional. The evidence needs to be sufficient for later review by management, internal audit, external audit, or a donor when applicable.

Study instruction:
- Use case studies to classify issues as missing evidence, control failure, or approved exception.

Key point:
- Missing evidence and failed controls are not the same problem.
- Each category requires a different response and escalation path.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
14. Q: Why should Finance verify that procurement approvals occurred before commitments were made?
A: Show answer
Authorization is most effective when it occurs before the organization becomes committed to the purchase. If a supplier has already delivered goods or started work before the proper approval was obtained, the organization may have created a difficult situation where payment is being requested for an obligation that was never properly authorized. Finance training should therefore emphasize timing. Staff should distinguish approval before procurement, approval of supplier selection, approval of the purchase order or contract, confirmation of receipt, and approval of payment. These are separate controls, not five signatures that can be collected after the fact. Late approvals may sometimes be allowed under a documented exception, but they should not become the normal operating model.

Study instruction:
- Map the timing of each approval in the organization’s procurement workflow and identify where retroactive approvals are prohibited or restricted.

Key point:
- The timing of approval matters as much as the existence of a signature.
- Retroactive approval should be treated as an exception, not a routine workaround.

Source links:
- UNDP: Segregation of Duties
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
15. Q: How would you train staff to recognize segregation-of-duties problems in procurement?
A: Show answer
Training should show that different responsibilities should be distributed so one person does not control the entire transaction from initiation to payment. Depending on organizational size, one person may request a purchase, another may perform or review procurement, another may confirm receipt, and Finance may review and process the payment. Small NGOs may have unavoidable overlaps, but those should be identified and compensated with alternative review controls. Staff should learn to spot combinations that create heightened risk, such as the same individual selecting the supplier, approving the purchase, confirming receipt, and directing payment. The objective is not to create bureaucracy for its own sake. It is to reduce the chance of error, favoritism, unauthorized purchases, or fraudulent payments by ensuring that critical stages are independently controlled.

Study instruction:
- Draw a simple responsibility matrix for procurement and identify incompatible duties.

Key point:
- No single person should control all major stages of a high-risk procurement transaction.
- Smaller offices should document compensating controls where full separation is impractical.

Source links:
- UNDP: Segregation of Duties
16. Q: Why should Finance understand conflict-of-interest controls in procurement?
A: Show answer
Procurement decisions can be affected by undisclosed personal or financial relationships, which creates both compliance and reputational risk. Finance staff do not normally investigate every relationship themselves, but they should know the organization’s conflict-of-interest requirements and recognize warning signs such as unusual supplier selection, repeated awards to connected parties, inconsistencies in declarations, or pressure to bypass normal procedures. When a potential conflict is identified, Finance should follow the designated escalation process rather than make its own informal determination. Training should explain that conflict controls protect both the organization and staff members by creating a structured way to disclose, assess, manage, and document potential conflicts.

Study instruction:
- Review the organization’s conflict-of-interest policy and identify where declarations, recusals, and approvals are documented.

Key point:
- Conflict-of-interest controls protect the integrity of supplier selection.
- Finance should escalate concerns through the defined governance process.

Source links:
- UNDP: Segregation of Duties
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
17. Q: How should staff check whether the payment is within the approved budget before disbursement?
A: Show answer
Procurement compliance and budget compliance are related but distinct controls. Even where a purchasing process is valid, the organization should confirm that the expenditure is charged to an authorized budget or funding source and that available funds are sufficient under the applicable rules. Finance should check the correct project, donor, account, cost center, and budget line and consider commitments already recorded but not yet paid. A payment that appears affordable on a cash basis may still create a budget overrun or violate a restricted funding condition. Staff should therefore assess both transaction authorization and financial availability before releasing cash, with stronger review where the expenditure is close to a threshold or where donor restrictions apply.

Study instruction:
- Practice reviewing budget, commitments, actuals, and remaining balance together before approving a procurement payment.

Key point:
- Procurement approval does not automatically mean budget capacity exists.
- Finance should check coding, funding eligibility, commitments, and available budget.

Source links:
- USAID ADS Chapter 303
- World Bank Procurement Framework
18. Q: Why should donor requirements be considered alongside the organization’s procurement policy?
A: Show answer
An NGO may have one internal procurement procedure but several donor agreements with additional conditions. A transaction can therefore satisfy the organization’s ordinary process and still fail a donor-specific requirement. Training should teach Finance to identify the applicable funding source and check whether donor rules add requirements concerning competition, source or nationality, prior approval, restricted goods or services, documentation, cost eligibility, or reporting. Staff should not assume that the least restrictive internal rule automatically governs. The correct control framework is the combination of internal policy, award terms, applicable donor instructions, and relevant law. Where the rules conflict or the requirement is unclear, the matter should be escalated before payment.

Study instruction:
- Create a donor compliance matrix showing procurement requirements that are stricter or different from standard internal procedures.

Key point:
- Donor terms can create additional procurement or payment conditions.
- Finance should identify the controlling requirement before disbursement.

Source links:
- USAID ADS Chapter 303
- World Bank Procurement Framework
19. Q: How should staff deal with urgent procurement requests?
A: Show answer
Urgency does not automatically eliminate procurement controls. Many policies provide specific emergency or expedited methods, but those methods generally still require documented justification, approval by an authorized person, supplier selection consistent with the emergency rule, and retention of supporting evidence. Finance should ask what made the purchase urgent, who approved use of the emergency method, what rule permits that method, and what evidence is required. Staff should be cautious when “urgent” purchases repeatedly arise from poor planning, because recurring emergencies can indicate a process weakness. The training message should be that emergency procurement is a defined procedure, not a general permission to bypass procurement rules whenever a team wants a faster payment.

Study instruction:
- Review the organization’s emergency procurement clause and identify the specific approvals and documentation it requires.

Key point:
- Emergency procurement should follow an approved exception method.
- Repeated urgency may indicate a planning or control problem.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
20. Q: How should Finance respond when a purchase appears to have been split to avoid a procurement threshold?
A: Show answer
Split procurement occurs when purchases that are economically related are separated so that each transaction stays below a threshold that would otherwise trigger a more demanding procurement method. Finance should look for patterns such as repeated purchases from the same supplier, similar goods or services ordered close together, recurring invoices for one project need, or transactions divided among multiple purchase requests without a clear business reason. Staff should not make a formal accusation based on one transaction. Instead, they should gather the evidence, compare it with the policy’s definition of aggregated or related purchases, and escalate the case for procurement or management review. A recurring pattern may require corrective action beyond a single payment decision.

Study instruction:
- Analyze several months of purchasing data to identify clusters that may indicate threshold avoidance or poor procurement planning.

Key point:
- Threshold compliance should be considered across related purchasing activity, not only one invoice at a time.
- Patterns should be investigated and escalated rather than assumed to be deliberate misconduct.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- World Bank Procurement Framework
21. Q: Why should finance staff be trained to recognize retroactive purchase orders?
A: Show answer
A purchase order created after goods or services have already been supplied does not provide the same preventive control as a PO issued before commitment. Retroactive documentation can conceal unauthorized procurement, weaken budget control, or make it difficult to demonstrate that supplier terms were approved in advance. Finance should therefore identify cases where the PO date, delivery date, invoice date, and approval date do not follow the expected sequence. Not every date mismatch proves a control failure; some systems or framework arrangements may legitimately produce different timing. The important point is to identify the exception and confirm that the organization’s policy permits the approach used and that appropriate corrective action or approval has been documented.

Study instruction:
- Test the sequence of dates in sample transactions and learn which date combinations should trigger review.

Key point:
- A PO created after performance may be a control exception rather than normal documentation.
- Date sequencing is a useful exception test for Finance.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
22. Q: How should staff distinguish procurement compliance from technical quality inspection?
A: Show answer
Finance should not be expected to become the technical expert on every good or service purchased. Procurement compliance asks whether the correct process was followed and documented; technical acceptance asks whether the goods or services meet the required specification or deliverable standard. The program, technical, engineering, warehouse, or other responsible function may be best placed to confirm technical quality. Finance should verify that the appropriate acceptance evidence exists and that the person confirming receipt is authorized and sufficiently independent. This distinction prevents Finance from either overstepping its role or paying without evidence that the organization accepted the supplier’s performance.

Study instruction:
- Identify who is authorized to confirm technical acceptance for each major procurement category.

Key point:
- Finance verifies that acceptance evidence exists; it does not necessarily perform the technical inspection itself.
- The acceptance function should belong to the appropriate operational or technical owner.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
23. Q: What should Finance check when a contract payment is based on milestones?
A: Show answer
Milestone payments require evidence that the specified milestone or event actually occurred. Finance should compare the invoice to the contract or purchase order and confirm that the responsible technical or project owner has certified the relevant deliverable. The certification should reference the defined milestone, not merely state that the supplier “completed the work.” Where the contract allows partial payment, retainage, advance payments, performance security, deductions, or other adjustments, Finance should verify the calculation against the agreed terms. The key training point is that the payment trigger is contractual performance, so the evidence must demonstrate that the contractual condition for payment has been met.

Study instruction:
- Review a sample service contract and identify exactly what evidence would trigger each payment milestone.

Key point:
- Milestone payments must be linked to defined contractual performance.
- Finance should verify the calculation and supporting certification before payment.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- USAID ADS Chapter 303
24. Q: How should Finance handle supplier invoices that exceed the purchase order amount?
A: Show answer
Finance should not simply pay the higher amount because the supplier has invoiced it. The difference should be explained and linked to an approved change, additional quantity, price adjustment, tax treatment, contract amendment, or other legitimate basis recognized by the governing terms. If an increase requires procurement or management approval, that approval should be obtained and retained before payment. An invoice exceeding the PO without explanation is both a payment control exception and a possible procurement control issue. Training should encourage staff to identify the reason for the variance and determine which person or function has authority to approve it rather than treating the PO as a flexible reference number.

Study instruction:
- Work through examples of quantity, price, tax, and scope changes and identify which require formal amendment or approval.

Key point:
- Invoice-over-PO variances need explanation and appropriate authorization.
- Procurement changes should be documented before payment where policy requires it.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
25. Q: Why should Finance review changes to supplier bank details before payment?
A: Show answer
Supplier bank-detail changes introduce a payment-fraud risk because a legitimate invoice can be redirected to an unauthorized account. The review should therefore distinguish procurement evidence from payment-master-data controls. Staff should follow the organization’s vendor-master procedure, verify the change through an approved independent channel, ensure the change was authorized, and avoid relying only on a request contained in an email or on the invoice itself. The payment should not be released simply because the supplier name matches the purchase order. A strong procurement-to-payment process considers both whether the organization owes the supplier and whether the payment destination is valid.

Study instruction:
- Review the vendor master change-control process and identify the independent verification step.

Key point:
- A valid procurement does not guarantee a valid payment destination.
- Bank-account changes require a separate control process.

Source links:
- UNDP: Segregation of Duties
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
26. Q: How would you train staff to identify inadequate supporting evidence without becoming overly restrictive?
A: Show answer
Training should distinguish essential evidence from unnecessary paperwork. The objective is to establish that the transaction is authorized, the procurement method is supported, the supplier’s claim is valid, the goods or services were received, the amount is correct, and payment is properly approved. Staff should avoid creating extra documentation that policy does not require simply because it “looks safer.” Excessive controls can delay programs and encourage workarounds. At the same time, a missing document that supports a critical control should not be waived merely to keep the payment moving. A useful training exercise is to provide realistic payment packages and ask staff to identify which missing items are material, which are administrative, and which require escalation.

Study instruction:
- Practice distinguishing material control evidence from duplicate or non-essential paperwork.

Key point:
- Good controls require sufficient evidence, not unlimited paperwork.
- Staff should focus on evidence that supports the key control objectives.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
27. Q: What procurement risks should Finance staff be able to recognize even when they are not investigators?
A: Show answer
Finance should be trained to recognize indicators that warrant escalation rather than trying to conduct a full investigation itself. Examples include repeated payments just below approval thresholds, identical invoices with altered references, unusual urgency, vendor changes immediately before payment, unsupported direct procurement, repeated use of the same supplier without documented rationale, inconsistent dates, conflicts of interest, payments for goods with no receiving evidence, or large variances from the contract. The purpose of awareness training is not to turn Finance into an investigative unit. It is to help staff identify transactions that deserve a second review and preserve the evidence rather than approving them automatically.

Study instruction:
- Build an exception library of procurement warning signs and define the escalation route for each category.

Key point:
- Finance should recognize warning signs and escalate them appropriately.
- Suspicious patterns require documented review, not unsupported accusations.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- UNDP: Segregation of Duties
28. Q: How should staff respond when a program team says that payment must be made immediately?
A: Show answer
Urgency should trigger focused review rather than automatic approval. Finance should identify the operational reason for urgency, confirm whether the procurement policy provides an emergency or expedited method, verify the approval authority, and document the applicable exception if one exists. It should also consider whether withholding payment would create a material operational consequence and whether an approved alternative process is available. Finance should communicate clearly what is missing and who can resolve it, rather than simply saying “Finance cannot pay.” This makes the control a problem-solving mechanism instead of a source of friction between Finance and program teams.

Study instruction:
- Practice explaining an urgent-payment hold in terms of the missing control, responsible owner, and available policy route.

Key point:
- Urgency should lead to an approved alternative route where available, not an undocumented bypass.
- Clear communication helps operational teams resolve the control issue quickly.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
29. Q: How would you train staff to handle direct or single-source procurement?
A: Show answer
Staff should understand that direct procurement is a specific procurement method, not merely the absence of competition. The policy should normally identify circumstances where direct selection is permitted, such as limited availability, compatibility, emergency conditions, continuation under an existing arrangement, or another defined justification. Finance should expect the appropriate written rationale, approval, value basis, and supporting evidence required by the policy. A weak explanation such as “the supplier is reliable” may not be enough if the policy requires a documented justification for bypassing competition. Training should focus on the requirement to demonstrate why the selected method was allowed and how the supplier and price were assessed under that method.

Study instruction:
- Compare examples of valid and weak direct-procurement justifications under your organization’s policy.

Key point:
- Direct procurement should be supported by a policy-based justification.
- The absence of competition requires stronger documentation of why the method was permitted.

Source links:
- World Bank: Evaluating Bids and Proposals
- UNDP Procurement at UNDP
30. Q: How should Finance verify that procurement was conducted against an appropriate specification or scope of work?
A: Show answer
The quality of the procurement process depends partly on whether the organization clearly defined what it intended to buy. Finance is not normally responsible for writing technical specifications, but it should be aware that the purchase order, contract, invoice, and acceptance evidence need to describe the same underlying transaction. Major discrepancies between the original specification and final invoice can indicate a scope change that requires approval. Training should therefore teach Finance to compare the commercial description and quantity with the approved request and contract and to escalate material changes. For services, the scope of work and deliverables should be sufficiently clear that the organization can demonstrate what performance was accepted before payment.

Study instruction:
- Practice tracing a procurement from specification to contract to invoice to acceptance evidence.

Key point:
- The purchase, contract, invoice, and acceptance evidence should relate to the same approved scope.
- Material scope changes should be authorized and documented.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
31. Q: Why should procurement commitments be recorded before payment?
A: Show answer
A purchase can create a financial commitment before cash is disbursed. Recording the commitment gives management visibility over funds already obligated by approved purchases and reduces the risk of treating the remaining budget as fully available. Finance can then compare approved commitments with actual payments and forecast future cash needs more accurately. This is particularly important for grants with fixed budget ceilings or projects operating close to their limits. Training should explain that “not yet paid” does not necessarily mean “not yet committed.” A strong control environment therefore follows the transaction from requisition through commitment and payment instead of looking only at bank movements.

Study instruction:
- Use a budget schedule that shows approved budget, commitments, actuals, and available balance separately.

Key point:
- Commitments provide visibility before cash is paid.
- Budget monitoring should consider obligations as well as historical expenditure.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Framework
32. Q: How can procurement and Finance work together to avoid year-end rush purchasing?
A: Show answer
A year-end spending rush often occurs when teams discover that budget remains available late in the period and attempt to accelerate purchases. Training should encourage earlier procurement planning, realistic implementation schedules, regular commitment reporting, and monthly review of unspent balances. Finance can provide visibility on budget availability and upcoming donor deadlines, while Procurement can provide information on the status of active purchasing processes. When the two functions share a forecast, they can identify transactions likely to miss the period and distinguish legitimate operational requirements from purchases being accelerated simply to use a budget. This supports better stewardship because procurement timing follows program need rather than the calendar alone.

Study instruction:
- Review monthly budget and procurement plans together and identify purchases that may miss the required implementation period.

Key point:
- Good procurement planning reduces year-end pressure and rushed decisions.
- Budget availability should not become a reason to weaken procurement controls.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- World Bank Procurement Framework
33. Q: What evidence should Finance retain for audit or donor review of procurement transactions?
A: Show answer
The retained evidence should allow an independent reviewer to reconstruct what was purchased, why it was needed, how the supplier was selected, who approved the transaction, what was delivered, how much was owed, and why the payment was valid. Depending on the applicable framework, the record may include procurement requests, bids or quotations, evaluation records, award decisions, contracts and amendments, invoices, inspection or delivery evidence, payment authorization, and approved exception documentation. Retention periods and exact documentation requirements vary by organization, donor, and law. Finance should therefore avoid assuming that keeping only the invoice and payment voucher is sufficient. The file should tell the story of the transaction from procurement decision to payment and remain retrievable for the required retention period.

Study instruction:
- Perform a mock audit using one procurement file and test whether another reviewer could reconstruct the transaction without asking staff for missing explanations.

Key point:
- Procurement files should allow an independent reviewer to reconstruct the transaction.
- Retention requirements should be checked against the applicable policy and award terms.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- USAID ADS Chapter 303
34. Q: How should Finance deal with procurement files that are stored in multiple systems?
A: Show answer
A fragmented document environment can make a compliant process appear incomplete because the evidence exists but is difficult to retrieve. Finance should establish a clear rule for the official procurement record, link the payment transaction to the procurement reference, and define where key documents are stored. Electronic systems can keep procurement documents in a central repository while Finance retains payment records in its accounting environment. The objective is not necessarily to duplicate every file in multiple locations but to make the audit trail traceable. Staff should know how to move from invoice number or purchase order number to the underlying procurement record and how to prove that the documents reviewed are the approved versions.

Study instruction:
- Map the document trail from accounting transaction to procurement repository and test retrieval using sample transaction references.

Key point:
- Evidence should be retrievable through a consistent reference structure.
- Central storage and traceable links reduce document-loss risk.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
35. Q: What should a practical procurement-payment checklist contain?
A: Show answer
A checklist should focus on the control questions that must be answered before cash is released. It can include: approved need or requisition; correct procurement method; evidence of competition or approved exception; supplier selection and approval; purchase order or contract; confirmation that goods or services were received; invoice accuracy; contract or PO match; budget and donor eligibility; correct tax treatment where relevant; approval authority; vendor bank details; and confirmation that required supporting documents are attached. The checklist should also include an exception field explaining what is missing, who owns the resolution, and whether payment is allowed while the issue is pending. A short, well-designed checklist is more useful than a long list of documents that staff tick without understanding the control purpose.

Study instruction:
- Build the checklist around control questions rather than simply creating a document inventory.

Key point:
- A good checklist links each document to a specific control objective.
- Exceptions should be visible rather than hidden in comments or email chains.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
36. Q: How could a finance workbook support pre-disbursement procurement controls?
A: Show answer
A workbook can provide a structured control layer without replacing the underlying procurement system or policy. It can contain transaction number, project, donor, supplier, procurement method, threshold category, PO or contract reference, invoice amount, receipt status, required documents, exception status, approver, and payment status. Power Query can consolidate procurement and payment data from multiple sources, while VBA can generate checklists, identify missing fields, prepare exception reports, or create review packs. The workbook should not automatically declare a transaction compliant simply because fields are completed. Human review remains necessary for judgmental issues such as procurement exceptions, conflicts, unusual variations, and donor-specific conditions.

Study instruction:
- Design a control tracker that automates completeness and matching checks but leaves policy judgments to authorized reviewers.

Key point:
- Automation should surface missing evidence and exceptions, not replace professional judgment.
- A structured control tracker improves consistency and traceability.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
37. Q: What exception fields should be tracked in a procurement-payment control log?
A: Show answer
Useful fields include transaction reference, supplier, project, amount, missing or inconsistent control, responsible owner, date identified, policy reference, action required, due date, escalation level, current status, resolution date, and final disposition. Additional fields can capture whether payment was held, partially released, or approved under a documented exception. The purpose is to convert scattered follow-up into a visible control process. Management can then see whether problems are isolated events or recurring patterns such as late POs, repeated missing receipts, frequent emergency procurement, or poor vendor documentation. The log should be reviewed periodically so recurring exceptions lead to process improvement rather than being closed individually without learning from the pattern.

Study instruction:
- Create a monthly exception dashboard showing volume, age, category, owner, and recurring root causes.

Key point:
- Exception logs should support both transaction resolution and process improvement.
- Recurring exceptions are evidence for management action, not just administrative statistics.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
38. Q: How should management measure whether staff are applying procurement controls correctly?
A: Show answer
Compliance should be measured through evidence rather than training attendance alone. Useful measures include the percentage of payment files with complete required procurement evidence, frequency of retroactive POs, number of unresolved procurement exceptions, percentage of payments held for missing documentation, recurring policy breaches, average exception resolution time, and the proportion of transactions subject to documented exception approval. Sampling is also important because a high apparent completion rate can hide repeated issues in particular teams or suppliers. Management should distinguish between process performance and deliberate non-compliance and investigate significant patterns rather than relying on one percentage as a complete control assessment.

Study instruction:
- Establish a small set of procurement-control KPIs and review trends by office, project, or process owner.

Key point:
- Training effectiveness should be measured by improved control behavior and evidence quality.
- Trend analysis can identify where additional training or process changes are needed.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
39. Q: How would you train non-finance staff who are responsible for initiating purchases?
A: Show answer
Their training should focus on the controls they own before Finance ever sees the transaction. They should understand how to define the need, obtain the correct approval, avoid premature commitments, use the correct procurement method, document supplier selection, communicate specifications accurately, and submit complete evidence. They also need to understand that Finance is not the team that can repair every procurement problem after the purchase has already happened. Training should use examples from their actual work—such as travel services, venue hire, consultants, medical supplies, transport, or office equipment—so the rules are linked to real decisions. Short scenario-based training is usually more useful than a long presentation of policy clauses without practical application.

Study instruction:
- Build role-specific scenarios for program, operations, logistics, and administrative staff involved in procurement initiation.

Key point:
- Preventive procurement responsibilities begin with the requesting team.
- Training should focus on decisions staff actually make.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- World Bank Procurement Framework
40. Q: How should training explain the difference between procurement compliance and payment approval?
A: Show answer
Procurement compliance asks whether the purchase was made through the appropriate process and under the required authority. Payment approval asks whether a valid amount is now due and can be paid from the appropriate funding source. These controls overlap but are not interchangeable. A fully compliant procurement can still have an incorrect invoice, incomplete delivery, or coding error. Conversely, a correct invoice does not prove that the procurement process was compliant. Training should explicitly teach staff to test both dimensions. This prevents the common mistake of using “the invoice matches the PO” as if it answered every question about the transaction.

Study instruction:
- Separate procurement questions and payment questions into two sections of the finance review checklist.

Key point:
- Procurement compliance and payment validity are separate control questions.
- Both must be addressed before disbursement.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
41. Q: How should staff handle a procurement file where the supplier delivered first and the contract was signed later?
A: Show answer
The sequence should be treated as an exception requiring review. Staff should establish why the supplier was permitted to begin work without the signed agreement, whether the policy allows such circumstances, whether there was an earlier valid commitment or framework arrangement, and who authorized the departure from the normal process. Finance should not retrospectively create a normal-looking file that obscures the actual sequence. The correct approach is to document what happened, determine the applicable policy treatment, and record any required management or donor approval. This preserves the integrity of the audit trail and allows management to address the underlying cause, such as planning delays or weak contract administration.

Study instruction:
- Practice documenting a chronological timeline for transactions with unusual procurement sequencing.

Key point:
- The file should reflect what actually happened, including exceptions.
- Retrospective paperwork should not conceal an earlier control failure.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
42. Q: What should Finance check when a procurement involves an advance payment to a supplier?
A: Show answer
An advance payment should be checked against the contract and applicable policy because it creates exposure before the organization has received the full goods or services. Finance should confirm the contractual basis for the advance, approval authority, amount, timing, and any required security or guarantees. It should also establish how the advance will be tracked and subsequently cleared against performance or delivery. A payment that is contractually permitted can still require strong monitoring because the risk continues until the supplier satisfies the relevant obligation. Training should teach staff to distinguish an authorized contractual advance from an unsupported prepayment and to maintain a schedule that shows open supplier advances and their settlement status.

Study instruction:
- Build a supplier-advance register linked to contract milestones and clearance evidence.

Key point:
- Supplier advances are controlled exposures that need separate monitoring.
- Contract terms and clearance arrangements should be checked before payment.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- USAID ADS Chapter 303
43. Q: How should staff review procurement transactions charged to restricted donor funds?
A: Show answer
Finance should first determine whether the cost is allowable under the specific award and then confirm whether the procurement process satisfies both internal and donor requirements. Restricted funding can create additional conditions concerning purpose, timing, prior approval, cost category, competitive procurement, or documentation. A transaction can therefore be correctly procured but still be ineligible for the donor budget, or it can be donor-eligible in principle but fail the organization’s procurement process. Training should teach staff to evaluate these conditions separately and retain evidence of both decisions. Where eligibility is unclear, the issue should be resolved before the cost is charged to the restricted funding source rather than corrected later during donor reporting.

Study instruction:
- Practice reviewing procurement invoices against both a grant budget and the applicable donor compliance matrix.

Key point:
- Procurement compliance and donor cost eligibility are separate tests.
- Restricted funds require transaction-level attention to the award conditions.

Source links:
- USAID ADS Chapter 303
- World Bank Procurement Framework
44. Q: How would you explain procurement controls to program managers without using excessive accounting terminology?
A: Show answer
The explanation should focus on the sequence of decisions rather than accounting labels: “Did we need it and authorize it? Did we choose the supplier using the required method? Did we agree the terms before committing? Did we receive what we ordered? Does the invoice match the agreement and the receipt? Is the cost allowed under the project or donor? Has the right person approved payment?” This gives non-finance managers a practical mental model. Finance can then explain that its role is to protect the organization and the program by checking those links before cash is released. The approach makes the controls easier to understand because each question connects directly to an operational action or piece of evidence.

Study instruction:
- Practice explaining procurement controls using operational questions that a program manager can answer directly.

Key point:
- Good financial training translates policy into practical decisions and evidence.
- Non-finance staff do not need to become accountants to follow strong procurement controls.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
45. Q: What are the most common mistakes Finance staff should avoid when checking procurement before disbursement?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include treating the invoice as sufficient evidence, checking only whether a PO exists rather than whether it was validly approved, assuming three quotations automatically satisfy every procurement rule, ignoring donor-specific requirements, approving based on verbal explanations, accepting missing documents without a documented exception, overlooking date sequencing, failing to verify receipt, and allowing repeated “urgent” transactions to bypass normal controls. Another mistake is overcorrecting by demanding documents that policy does not require, which can create unnecessary delay. Effective training should teach staff to focus on the control objective, the applicable rule, the evidence required, and the appropriate response when something is missing.

Study instruction:
- Use real or anonymized payment files to identify both under-control and over-control behaviors.

Key point:
- Strong review is neither automatic approval nor unnecessary bureaucracy.
- Staff should apply the applicable rule consistently and document exceptions clearly.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
46. Q: How should Finance handle disagreement with Procurement over whether a payment is ready?
A: Show answer
The discussion should focus on the specific policy requirement and evidence rather than on which department is “right.” Finance should state the control question, identify the document or condition that is missing, and refer to the applicable policy or donor rule. Procurement should have an opportunity to explain the process and provide additional evidence or identify an approved exception. If the disagreement remains material, it should be escalated to the designated manager or control owner. The transaction should not be approved simply because the operational team is under pressure, but neither should Finance impose a requirement that the governing policy does not contain. A documented escalation process helps resolve disagreements consistently and keeps the issue from becoming a personal conflict.

Study instruction:
- Practice resolving a disputed payment using evidence, policy references, and defined escalation rather than informal arguments.

Key point:
- Policy and evidence should anchor disagreements between Finance and Procurement.
- Material unresolved issues should move through a defined escalation route.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
47. Q: How would you train staff to respond when a procurement control fails after the payment has already been made?
A: Show answer
Once a payment has been made, the objective changes from prevention to correction, risk assessment, and learning. Staff should record the exception, identify which control failed, determine whether the expenditure is financially or donor eligible, assess whether a recovery or reclassification is needed, and escalate where policy requires management or donor notification. The issue should then be analyzed for root cause. For example, a missing quotation caused by one employee may require targeted coaching, while repeated retroactive purchasing may indicate a structural planning problem. Post-payment correction should not be used as a substitute for pre-payment controls, but it should provide a disciplined way to address problems that were not caught in time.

Study instruction:
- Build a post-payment exception process that covers correction, escalation, root cause, and preventative action.

Key point:
- A failed control requires both transaction correction and process learning.
- Repeated exceptions should trigger broader management action.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
48. Q: How can management use procurement-payment exceptions to improve training?
A: Show answer
Exception data should be analyzed by root cause rather than simply counted. If most exceptions relate to missing delivery evidence, the training should focus on receiving documentation. If they relate to wrong procurement methods, staff may need threshold and method training. If emergency purchases are frequent, management may need to address procurement planning rather than repeat the same compliance briefing. Finance can summarize the exception categories, responsible process owners, age, value, and recurrence rate and use that information in monthly management meetings. This turns the control process into a feedback loop: transactions reveal weaknesses, management addresses the weaknesses, and future transactions are monitored to see whether the problem is improving.

Study instruction:
- Link each recurring exception category to a specific training, process, or system intervention.

Key point:
- Exception analysis should drive targeted corrective action.
- Training is more effective when it responds to actual process failures.

Source links:
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
49. Q: What should a monthly management review of procurement controls include?
A: Show answer
A useful review can show total procurement value, number of transactions, outstanding commitments, payments held for control reasons, exception volume, aging of unresolved cases, direct or emergency procurement, retroactive documentation, repeated supplier issues, and significant budget or donor concerns. Management should not focus only on whether Finance processed payments quickly. It should also consider whether the organization is procuring in a planned, compliant, and timely way. Trend information can reveal systemic weaknesses before they become audit findings. The discussion should end with clear actions, owners, and deadlines, especially where the same exception is recurring across multiple projects or offices.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a monthly procurement-control dashboard and use it to assign corrective actions rather than only report statistics.

Key point:
- Management reporting should cover both efficiency and control quality.
- Recurring exceptions should result in assigned actions and follow-up.

Source links:
- World Bank Procurement Framework
- UNDP Procurement Guiding Principles
50. Q: What would a strong end-to-end training exercise for pre-disbursement procurement look like?
A: Show answer
A strong exercise should give staff a realistic transaction file containing a requisition, procurement method evidence, supplier quotations or bid records, evaluation or selection documentation, PO or contract, receiving evidence, invoice, budget information, and payment approval. The learner should trace the transaction in sequence, identify missing or inconsistent evidence, determine which issues can be resolved administratively, identify which issues require escalation, and decide whether payment can proceed under the applicable policy. The exercise should include at least one legitimate exception, one date inconsistency, one invoice variance, and one donor-specific condition so staff must apply judgment rather than simply tick boxes. The training can then be followed by a short debrief explaining why each decision was taken and how the evidence supports it.

Study instruction:
- Complete a full simulated payment review from requisition to disbursement and document every decision and exception.

Key point:
- Practical case-based training builds judgment better than policy memorization alone.
- The final test is whether staff can connect policy, evidence, timing, and escalation before cash is released.

Source links:
- CIPS: From Requisition to Payment
- World Bank Procurement Regulations
- USAID ADS Chapter 303
