Table 7
Financial Checklists for Non-Finance Managers
1. Q: How would you train non-finance managers to use financial checklists without expecting them to become accountants?
A: Show answer
The purpose of training should be to help managers make sound operational decisions and provide the facts Finance needs for accurate accounting. A checklist should therefore focus on matters managers can reasonably know and control: whether the activity is within an approved budget, whether the expenditure has a valid business or programme purpose, whether the required approval was obtained, whether goods or services were actually received, whether donor or grant conditions apply, and whether supporting documents are complete and submitted on time. Finance should remain responsible for technical accounting judgments such as account classification, accrual treatment, tax treatment, capitalization, and financial statement presentation. Training should make the boundary visible: managers confirm operational facts; Finance applies accounting rules and performs technical review. This creates shared accountability without turning program managers into accountants.

Study instruction:
- Learn the difference between operational responsibility and technical accounting responsibility.
- Practice explaining which checklist items belong to managers and which require Finance review.

Key point:
- Managers need practical financial literacy, not full accounting expertise.
- Finance should retain responsibility for technical accounting judgments.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
2. Q: What should a financial checklist mean to a non-finance manager?
A: Show answer
A financial checklist should be understood as a decision-support and control tool rather than an accounting examination. It prompts the manager to confirm facts before an organization commits or reports resources. For example, a manager may be asked whether the purchase is necessary for the approved activity, whether budget is available, whether the procurement process was followed, whether the supplier delivered what was ordered, and whether the supporting documentation is complete. The manager should not be expected to determine whether an item is an accrued expense, deferred income, or a capital asset unless that technical responsibility has specifically been delegated and training supports it. A well-designed checklist helps managers identify issues early and gives Finance a reliable evidence trail for the accounting work that follows.

Study instruction:
- Treat the checklist as a control and decision-support instrument rather than a substitute for accounting knowledge.

Key point:
- The checklist should help confirm facts, approvals, and risks.
- It should not transfer technical accounting responsibility to operational staff.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
3. Q: Why should Finance distinguish operational checks from technical accounting checks?
A: Show answer
The distinction prevents control responsibilities from becoming unclear. A program manager can usually determine whether an activity happened, whether a supplier delivered, whether a participant list exists, whether a service was necessary, or whether the expenditure relates to the intended project. Finance is better placed to determine how that verified transaction should be recognized, classified, reconciled, and reported. If the two responsibilities are mixed, managers may make accounting judgments without adequate knowledge, while Finance may be forced to make operational assertions it cannot independently verify. A strong checklist therefore separates questions such as “Did we receive the service?” from questions such as “How should this transaction be accounted for?” The first is primarily operational evidence; the second is a technical accounting matter. Both are necessary, but they should not be confused.

Study instruction:
- Review sample checklist questions and classify each as operational, accounting, compliance, or shared responsibility.

Key point:
- Clear role boundaries strengthen accountability.
- Operational confirmation and accounting treatment are related but different tasks.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
4. Q: What financial concepts should non-finance managers understand before using checklists?
A: Show answer
Managers should understand a limited set of concepts that directly affect their decisions. These normally include budget, actual expenditure, commitments, forecast, variance, restricted funding, authorization, procurement requirements, supporting documentation, cash implications, and financial accountability. They should know that an approved budget is not unlimited authority to spend without following procedures, that an expenditure can be committed before cash is paid, and that a favorable variance may require investigation rather than automatically being treated as good performance. They should also understand that financial reports summarize activities and decisions they control operationally. The training objective is not to teach journal entries; it is to give managers enough financial literacy to interpret information, identify exceptions, ask informed questions, and take timely action when programme performance and financial performance diverge.

Study instruction:
- Build a one-page glossary of the financial terms managers use most frequently.

Key point:
- Financial literacy should focus on decisions managers actually make.
- Understanding budget, actuals, commitments, forecasts, and variances is usually more useful than learning bookkeeping mechanics.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
5. Q: How should the checklist show budget availability without requiring managers to perform accounting calculations?
A: Show answer
The checklist should present budget information in an understandable management format. Instead of asking a manager to calculate ledger balances, Finance can provide the approved budget, expenditure to date, known commitments, remaining available balance, and a simple indication of whether the proposed activity appears affordable. The manager should then confirm whether the proposed expenditure is appropriate for the approved workplan and whether any known operational commitments are missing from the financial information. Finance remains responsible for validating the underlying accounting records. This approach lets managers use budget information without requiring them to reconcile accounting systems manually. It also helps prevent the misconception that “budget remaining” automatically means “permission to spend.” Authorization, donor restrictions, procurement requirements, and timing still need to be considered.

Study instruction:
- Practice reading a budget position without recalculating the accounting records behind it.

Key point:
- Managers should interpret budget availability, not rebuild the ledger.
- Budget availability is only one part of the spending decision.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
6. Q: How would you teach managers to understand budget versus actual expenditure?
A: Show answer
Training should begin with the simple idea that budget versus actual compares what was planned with what has happened financially to date. Managers should learn to ask whether the comparison period is appropriate, whether the expenditure relates to the expected activity, and whether differences are temporary, timing-related, structural, or caused by an error. They should avoid treating every variance as a failure. A project may underspend because an activity was delayed, while another may overspend because a major implementation stage occurred earlier than planned. The manager’s role is to explain the operational reason and determine the required action. Finance should support the analysis by ensuring that the underlying expenditure is correctly recorded and that the comparison uses consistent dates and categories.

Study instruction:
- Take a sample budget-versus-actual report and explain the operational reason for each material variance.

Key point:
- Variance analysis is about understanding causes and consequences, not just differences in numbers.
- Managers provide the operational explanation while Finance validates financial accuracy.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
7. Q: How should non-finance managers be trained to distinguish a timing variance from a real financial problem?
A: Show answer
A timing variance occurs when spending happens earlier or later than the original plan, while a substantive variance may indicate that the activity itself is costing more or less than expected. Managers should first compare the financial variance with the implementation schedule. If procurement was delayed, an underspend may simply reflect timing. If supplier prices increased substantially or activity volumes changed, the same variance may represent a more significant issue. The checklist can ask three practical questions: What was planned? What actually happened? What is now expected to happen? This moves the discussion from historic numbers to current management action. Finance can then determine whether accounting adjustments, revised forecasts, reallocation approvals, or donor notifications are required.

Study instruction:
- Classify sample variances as timing, scope, price, volume, classification, or other causes.

Key point:
- The first step in variance analysis is identifying the cause.
- Timing differences should not automatically be treated as permanent performance problems.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
8. Q: What should managers learn about commitments before approving new expenditure?
A: Show answer
Managers should understand that money can be committed before it appears as paid expenditure. A purchase order, signed contract, approved supplier engagement, or other binding commitment may create a future financial obligation even though the invoice has not yet been paid. If managers look only at actual expenditure, they may believe more budget is available than really is. A useful checklist should therefore ask whether the proposed activity is already covered by an existing commitment, whether additional contracts or purchase orders are pending, and whether the latest forecast includes them. Finance can maintain the official commitment records and reconcile them to the accounting system. Managers then provide the operational information needed to identify commitments that Finance may not yet see.

Study instruction:
- Review examples of expenditures that are paid, committed, planned, or only proposed.

Key point:
- Actual expenditure and financial commitments are not the same thing.
- Managers should consider future obligations before committing additional resources.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
9. Q: How should a checklist teach managers to consider cash implications?
A: Show answer
Managers do not need to manage the bank account, but they should understand that approved activities have cash implications. A project may have sufficient annual budget but still require careful cash planning because several large payments could become due at the same time. Managers should therefore tell Finance about expected procurement milestones, partner transfers, major events, travel cycles, payroll-related activity, and other significant cash requirements. A checklist can include a simple question asking whether the planned activity creates a significant or unusual cash requirement that Finance needs to know about. Finance then integrates that information into cash forecasting. This helps the organization avoid treating budget approval as the same thing as immediate cash availability.

Study instruction:
- Identify which program activities could create significant short-term cash demands.

Key point:
- Budget authorization and cash availability are different management concepts.
- Operational teams should provide timely information about major future cash needs.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
10. Q: Why should managers understand that donor restrictions can affect spending decisions?
A: Show answer
Donor funding may have conditions relating to activities, locations, beneficiaries, time periods, budget categories, or approval requirements. A manager therefore cannot treat every available amount as interchangeable operating cash. The checklist should prompt managers to confirm that proposed expenditure relates to an eligible activity and remains within the applicable funding conditions. Finance should maintain the technical donor coding, grant accounting, and reporting treatment, while managers provide evidence about what the expenditure was actually for. Training should also explain that a transaction may be operationally reasonable but still unsuitable for a particular restricted grant. Early consultation with Finance is preferable to correcting an ineligible transaction after it has already been incurred.

Study instruction:
- Review a grant budget and identify the operational decisions that could create donor-eligibility risks.

Key point:
- Availability of funding does not necessarily mean unrestricted ability to spend.
- Managers should understand the operational effect of donor restrictions.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
11. Q: How should a financial checklist address business purpose?
A: Show answer
The checklist should ask the manager to confirm why the organization incurred or intends to incur the cost and how it connects to an approved objective or activity. This is different from asking whether the transaction has the correct account code. A clear business or program purpose helps Finance determine appropriate accounting and donor treatment later. It also supports auditability because a reviewer can understand why the transaction occurred. The manager should be able to explain the operational reason in ordinary language, without using accounting terminology. For example, “venue hire for the approved community training scheduled for 15 October” is more useful than simply writing “meeting expense.” The purpose statement should be proportionate to the transaction but sufficiently clear to support later review.

Study instruction:
- Rewrite vague transaction descriptions into clear statements of operational purpose.

Key point:
- Managers should establish why the organization needed the expenditure.
- Clear business purpose supports accounting, donor reporting, and audit review.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
12. Q: What evidence should managers be expected to review before signing a financial checklist?
A: Show answer
The evidence depends on the transaction, but managers should normally verify the documents that demonstrate the underlying operational facts. This might include a purchase request, quotation or procurement record, purchase order, contract, delivery note, attendance record, service completion evidence, activity report, approval record, or invoice. Managers should not be expected to validate every accounting attribute if that is Finance’s role. Their responsibility is to confirm that the evidence supports what they are attesting to. The checklist should therefore avoid vague wording such as “documents checked” and instead identify the relevant evidence. This creates a clearer audit trail and helps managers understand that signing a checklist is an accountability action rather than a routine administrative formality.

Study instruction:
- Build evidence requirements around the operational assertion each manager is being asked to confirm.

Key point:
- Evidence should directly support the fact the manager is confirming.
- Checklist signatures should represent meaningful review, not automatic approval.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
13. Q: How should managers be trained to distinguish approval from verification?
A: Show answer
Approval and verification are related but different. Approval means an authorized person permits a transaction or activity to proceed within the organization’s authority structure. Verification means someone checks that a stated fact is supported, such as confirming that goods were received or an activity occurred. A manager may perform both roles for some transactions, but they should not be assumed to be identical. The checklist can make the distinction visible by using separate statements, such as “I confirm that the service was received” and “I approve the expenditure within my delegated authority.” Where segregation of duties is required, different people should perform those steps. Finance should explain the organization’s approval matrix and control structure so managers understand why certain signatures cannot simply be combined for convenience.

Study instruction:
- Practice identifying whether each checklist action is an approval, verification, review, reconciliation, or accounting action.

Key point:
- Approval authorizes; verification confirms facts.
- Segregation of duties may require different people to perform different control steps.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
14. Q: How can Finance teach managers what they are accountable for without making them responsible for technical accounting?
A: Show answer
Accountability should be linked to the decisions and facts within the manager’s authority. A program manager can reasonably be held accountable for whether activities were planned, whether resources were used for approved purposes, whether services were delivered, whether required operational documentation exists, and whether problems were reported. Finance can remain accountable for the accuracy of accounting records, application of accounting standards, reconciliations, financial statement preparation, and technical classifications. Training should explicitly state that managers are not being asked to “do Finance’s job.” They are being asked to provide reliable operational information and exercise their delegated authority properly. This creates a control environment in which both functions understand their own responsibilities and the points where cooperation is required.

Study instruction:
- Map key financial-control responsibilities to the people who actually have authority over the related decisions.

Key point:
- Accountability should follow authority and control over the underlying activity.
- Technical accounting responsibility should remain clearly assigned to Finance.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
15. Q: What common mistake occurs when non-finance managers are given overly technical financial checklists?
A: Show answer
An overly technical checklist can cause managers to focus on language they do not understand instead of the operational risks they can control. Terms such as accruals, provisions, deferred income, impairment, capitalization, and journal classification may be important, but they should not be placed on a program manager’s checklist unless the manager has a defined responsibility for them. Excessive technical content can also encourage superficial sign-off because the manager cannot meaningfully evaluate what is being asked. A better approach is to translate technical requirements into operational questions. For example, instead of asking a manager whether an expense satisfies a technical recognition criterion, the checklist can ask when the service was actually received and whether evidence of completion exists. Finance can then use that information for the accounting judgment.

Study instruction:
- Convert technical accounting questions into operational evidence questions where appropriate.

Key point:
- A technically sophisticated checklist can be a poor control if users cannot understand it.
- Good design translates accounting requirements into observable operational facts.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
16. Q: How would you train managers to identify when they should involve Finance?
A: Show answer
Training should give managers clear escalation triggers rather than expecting them to decide everything themselves. Examples include an unusual transaction, uncertainty over donor eligibility, a significant unplanned expenditure, a suspected duplicate payment, a missing supporting document, an unusual supplier arrangement, a proposed budget reallocation, a major forecast change, a possible conflict of interest, or a transaction that crosses organizational or funding boundaries. The message should be that asking Finance early is a control behavior, not an admission of failure. Managers should know who to contact, what information to provide, and what deadlines apply. Finance can reinforce this by responding consistently and explaining the reason behind its technical advice rather than simply saying “Finance policy does not allow it.”

Study instruction:
- Create a short escalation matrix showing which types of issues require immediate Finance involvement.

Key point:
- Managers need clear escalation triggers, not instructions to solve technical issues alone.
- Early Finance involvement can prevent expensive corrections later.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
17. Q: How should managers be trained to respond to an overspend?
A: Show answer
An overspend should trigger investigation rather than an immediate assumption that someone breached a rule. The manager should first establish whether the variance is caused by timing, higher prices, higher activity volumes, scope changes, foreign exchange, coding errors, omitted commitments, or an actual control failure. The checklist can ask what was originally planned, what changed, how much the difference is, whether the change is temporary or permanent, and what action is proposed. Finance should validate the financial data and advise on accounting or budget-control implications. Depending on policy, management may need to approve a reallocation, revised forecast, donor notification, or corrective action. The essential training point is that an overspend is information requiring explanation and response.

Study instruction:
- Analyze an overspend example and separate its financial cause, operational cause, and required management response.

Key point:
- Overspending should be investigated by cause, not merely reported as a negative number.
- Managers should act on operational causes while Finance supports financial analysis and control.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
18. Q: How should managers respond to significant underspending?
A: Show answer
Underspending may indicate delayed implementation, lower-than-expected activity volume, procurement problems, staffing gaps, unrealistic budgets, or an opportunity to deliver additional outputs. Managers should therefore avoid treating underspending as automatically positive. The checklist should ask whether planned activities were completed, whether future spending is still expected, whether the forecast has been updated, and whether the underspend creates a risk to project results or funding conditions. Finance can help distinguish accounting timing issues from real implementation differences. If donor deadlines or restrictions are involved, the manager may need to consider whether a revised plan or donor approval is required. The purpose of the review is to connect financial performance with programme performance rather than focusing only on whether money was spent.

Study instruction:
- Compare financial underspending with implementation progress and identify cases where low expenditure could signal an operational problem.

Key point:
- Underspending can be either temporary or a sign of under-delivery.
- Financial performance should be interpreted alongside programme performance.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
19. Q: How can a checklist support accurate forecasting by non-finance managers?
A: Show answer
Forecasting should use the manager’s knowledge of what will actually happen, not simply extend historical spending forward. The checklist can ask about activities still to be delivered, expected procurement, staffing changes, contracts, travel, partner transfers, major events, known delays, and emerging risks. Managers do not need to calculate the entire forecast model. They need to provide timely information about operational events that will affect financial outcomes. Finance can incorporate those inputs into the formal forecast, test assumptions, and identify inconsistencies. Managers should also understand that a forecast is not a promise of exact expenditure; it is a current estimate that should improve as new information becomes available. Frequent, disciplined updates are more useful than an annual forecast that is rarely revisited.

Study instruction:
- Practice updating a forecast based on actual implementation changes and new operational commitments.

Key point:
- Managers provide forward-looking operational information; Finance consolidates and analyzes the financial forecast.
- Forecast accuracy depends heavily on timely management input.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
20. Q: What should managers do when a forecast is repeatedly inaccurate?
A: Show answer
Repeated forecast inaccuracies should be treated as a process issue rather than simply blaming the person preparing the forecast. The organization should examine whether assumptions are documented, whether managers receive current financial information, whether commitments are captured, whether implementation plans change frequently, and whether the forecasting horizon is appropriate. A checklist can require managers to state the main assumptions behind major forecast movements and identify events that could cause further change. Finance can compare previous forecasts with actual outcomes and show where the largest errors occurred. This creates a learning loop. The purpose is not to demand perfect prediction but to make assumptions visible, improve information flow, and identify recurring sources of error.

Study instruction:
- Compare prior forecasts with actual results and identify the assumptions that caused the largest differences.

Key point:
- Forecast quality improves when assumptions and causes of error are documented.
- Repeated forecast errors should lead to process improvement rather than routine blame.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
21. Q: How should managers be trained to identify unexplained transactions?
A: Show answer
Managers are often best placed to recognize whether a transaction makes operational sense, even when they cannot determine its accounting classification. A checklist can present selected unusual or unexplained items and ask the manager whether the transaction relates to a known activity, supplier, event, employee, or project. If the answer is unclear, the manager should provide context or escalate the item to Finance. The goal is not for managers to investigate accounting records independently, but to use their operational knowledge to help resolve exceptions. Finance can then assess coding, supporting documentation, duplicate risks, reconciliations, and accounting treatment. This is especially useful where Finance sees a transaction in the ledger but cannot determine its underlying business purpose from the documentation alone.

Study instruction:
- Review sample unexplained transactions and identify what operational evidence would help Finance resolve each one.

Key point:
- Managers can provide context that Finance cannot obtain from the ledger alone.
- Unexplained items should remain visible until their purpose and treatment are established.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
22. Q: Why should checklists include documentation and evidence requirements?
A: Show answer
A financial decision that cannot later be supported is difficult to defend, even when the original decision was reasonable. Checklists should therefore make the minimum evidence expectations explicit. Managers need to know which documents they are responsible for obtaining or confirming, when the documents must be submitted, and what to do when an expected document is unavailable. The requirement should be proportionate: routine low-value transactions may need less evidence than major contracts or sensitive expenditures. Finance can define document-retention and accounting requirements, while managers ensure the operational evidence exists. This shared approach prevents the common problem where Finance discovers missing documentation only at month-end or during an audit, when obtaining reliable evidence may be much more difficult.

Study instruction:
- For several transaction types, identify the minimum evidence needed to prove purpose, authorization, delivery, and payment legitimacy.

Key point:
- Evidence requirements should be clear, proportionate, and established before problems arise.
- Managers play an important role in preserving operational evidence.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
23. Q: How should managers handle missing documentation without simply ignoring it?
A: Show answer
A missing document should be treated as an exception requiring explanation and resolution. The checklist should identify what is missing, why it is unavailable, who is responsible for obtaining or replacing it, and the deadline for resolution. Managers should not create or approve substitute documentation that falsely represents what happened. Where policy permits alternative evidence, Finance should determine what is acceptable and record the exception appropriately. Repeated missing documents may indicate a broader process weakness that requires training or control redesign. The objective is not to punish every missing item but to maintain an evidence trail and prevent exceptions from silently becoming normal practice.

Study instruction:
- Practice documenting a missing-supporting-document exception without converting it into a misleading substitute record.

Key point:
- Missing evidence should be logged, explained, and resolved or formally accepted under policy.
- Repeated documentation failures should trigger process improvement.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
24. Q: How should a financial checklist deal with procurement implications?
A: Show answer
Program managers often know what the organization needs before Finance sees the financial transaction, so they should understand the connection between programme requirements and procurement controls. The checklist can ask whether the purchase is included in the approved activity, whether the required procurement route was followed, whether specifications were appropriate, whether conflicts of interest were addressed where required, and whether receipt or service completion can be demonstrated. Managers do not need to become procurement officers, but they should understand that financial authority does not override procurement requirements. Finance can verify the financial implications, while procurement specialists or authorized officers handle technical procurement compliance where applicable. The checklist should make these interfaces visible rather than treating finance, procurement, and programme controls as separate worlds.

Study instruction:
- Map a sample purchase from programme need through procurement, approval, receipt, and financial recording.

Key point:
- Procurement and financial controls interact throughout the transaction lifecycle.
- Managers should understand which procurement steps they control and which require specialist review.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
25. Q: How can financial checklists improve communication between program managers and Finance?
A: Show answer
A common checklist creates a shared language for recurring financial conversations. Instead of Finance asking broad questions such as “Why is the budget off?”, the manager can be asked about specific implementation events, commitments, delays, procurement issues, or changes in activity volume. Likewise, managers can use the checklist to raise questions about financial data that appears inconsistent with what they know operationally. The value is not the form itself but the discipline it creates around the conversation. Regular checklist review should lead to a short exchange about exceptions, actions, deadlines, and responsibilities. Finance should also explain unusual accounting conclusions in operational language where possible. This makes financial management a two-way information process rather than a one-way request for documents.

Study instruction:
- Use the checklist as an agenda for a short Finance-programme review meeting and identify the issues that require action.

Key point:
- Checklists can structure better communication when they focus on decisions and exceptions.
- Financial communication should connect numbers to operational facts.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
26. Q: How should managers prepare for a monthly financial review meeting?
A: Show answer
Before the meeting, managers should review the latest budget-versus-actual information, forecast, significant commitments, unresolved documentation issues, and major operational changes that could affect financial performance. The checklist should identify a limited number of questions requiring managerial action rather than reproducing the entire ledger. Managers should be prepared to explain material variances, confirm the status of delayed activities, identify emerging spending pressures, and update expected future costs. Finance should bring validated financial information and highlight areas requiring management attention. The meeting should finish with agreed actions, responsible people, and deadlines. This creates a recurring management rhythm in which financial information is used to manage performance rather than discussed only after month-end reports have already been finalized.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a sample monthly financial review pack and identify which issues should be discussed with management first.

Key point:
- Monthly review meetings should focus on decisions, exceptions, and forward-looking actions.
- The checklist should reduce meeting time spent searching for basic information.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
27. Q: How should managers use dashboards without becoming dependent on financial specialists?
A: Show answer
A dashboard should show information managers need to act: budget utilization, major variances, forecast changes, commitments, funding status, implementation progress, overdue actions, and significant risks. Training should explain what each indicator means, what period it covers, and what action it should trigger. Managers should not need to understand every formula behind the dashboard, but they should know its limitations and recognize when to ask Finance for supporting detail. A good dashboard distinguishes between information for awareness and indicators that require action. It should also make data freshness visible so managers do not assume that an old report reflects the current position. The goal is informed management use of financial data, not technical dependence on the person who built the dashboard.

Study instruction:
- Select five dashboard indicators and write the management action each one should trigger.

Key point:
- Dashboards should support decisions, not merely display numbers.
- Users need enough knowledge to interpret indicators and recognize when further analysis is required.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
28. Q: How should Finance explain materiality to non-finance managers?
A: Show answer
Materiality is not simply a fixed number that applies identically to every situation. Managers should understand that the significance of an issue depends on amount, context, risk, funding conditions, reporting impact, and the possibility that a small issue may reveal a larger control weakness. A small unsupported expense may not materially affect the financial statements but could still indicate a serious segregation-of-duties or fraud risk. Conversely, a large timing difference may be temporary and well understood. The checklist should therefore avoid telling managers to ignore every issue below a certain amount. Instead, it can identify thresholds for routine escalation while preserving the ability to escalate unusual or sensitive matters regardless of amount. Finance should provide the technical assessment of reporting materiality.

Study instruction:
- Compare examples where numerical size and control significance lead to different conclusions about escalation.

Key point:
- Financial size and control significance are not always the same.
- Materiality decisions should consider context, risk, and reporting implications.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
29. Q: What should a manager do when they disagree with Finance about a financial issue?
A: Show answer
The disagreement should be resolved through evidence, policy, and clearly defined roles rather than through informal authority. The manager should explain the operational facts and provide the evidence supporting the proposed treatment. Finance should explain the relevant accounting, funding, compliance, or control requirement and distinguish mandatory requirements from judgmental preferences. Where disagreement remains, the issue should be escalated to the appropriate decision-maker under the organization’s governance structure. The checklist can record the issue, evidence considered, responsible decision-maker, and final action without turning the checklist into a substitute for formal policy approval. This approach recognizes that Finance may need information from the program team while the program team may need technical guidance from Finance.

Study instruction:
- Practice documenting a disagreement as a factual issue, a policy question, or an accounting judgment requiring escalation.

Key point:
- Disagreement should be resolved through evidence, policy, and escalation routes.
- Operational facts and technical accounting advice should be kept distinct.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
30. Q: How should checklists handle competing program priorities?
A: Show answer
A manager may face several urgent programme needs at the same time, but financial controls should not disappear simply because operational pressure is high. The checklist can help by identifying which decisions require evidence, approval, procurement review, funding confirmation, or Finance escalation before resources are committed. Where priorities compete, managers should compare the operational urgency with available budget, donor restrictions, commitments, cash implications, and implementation objectives. Finance can support the manager by showing the financial consequences of alternative options rather than simply stating whether a cost is affordable. This makes the checklist a decision framework rather than a rigid administrative obstacle. The manager remains responsible for prioritizing within their authority, while Finance helps make the financial consequences visible.

Study instruction:
- Compare two competing programme requests and identify the financial information needed before deciding how to proceed.

Key point:
- Financial controls should support prioritization rather than operate as isolated paperwork.
- Managers need financial information to understand the consequences of competing choices.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
31. Q: How can Finance teach managers to recognize financial risk without overwhelming them?
A: Show answer
Training should focus on a small number of risk indicators directly connected to managers’ responsibilities. These may include spending without authorization, transactions outside approved activities, unexplained budget movements, unusually large or urgent purchases, repeated missing documentation, conflicts of interest, supplier anomalies, unexplained advances, delayed reporting, and significant forecast changes. Managers do not need a complete enterprise risk framework for every decision. They need to know which circumstances should cause them to pause, gather evidence, or escalate. A simple checklist can combine risk indicators with clear actions: continue, obtain additional evidence, consult Finance, obtain approval, or escalate. This makes financial risk practical rather than abstract and helps managers recognize that controls exist to protect programme resources and objectives.

Study instruction:
- Create a risk-and-response table linking common financial warning signs to the appropriate management action.

Key point:
- Managers need practical risk signals and clear responses.
- Risk training is most effective when connected to real decisions and observable warning signs.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
32. Q: What common mistakes should managers avoid when completing financial checklists?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include signing without reading, assuming Finance will fix missing information later, treating the budget as spending permission, ignoring commitments, confirming receipt without checking delivery, submitting incomplete evidence, using vague descriptions, overlooking donor restrictions, and failing to report known changes to forecasts. Another mistake is trying to answer technical accounting questions confidently when the manager does not have enough information. The checklist should make these failure points visible and explain what managers should do instead. Training should use realistic examples because people learn better when they can see how a seemingly minor shortcut can create downstream accounting, donor, audit, or control problems. The objective is to create thoughtful completion rather than perfect-looking forms.

Study instruction:
- Review intentionally flawed checklist examples and identify which control failures they contain.

Key point:
- Most checklist problems arise from weak review behavior, unclear responsibilities, or incomplete evidence.
- The remedy is better control design and training, not simply more signatures.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
33. Q: How should Finance train managers to deal with late submission of financial information?
A: Show answer
Late submissions can disrupt reconciliations, month-end closing, donor reporting, and forecasting. Managers should understand the operational consequences of missing deadlines rather than viewing Finance deadlines as administrative preferences. The checklist can include due dates for supporting documents, activity confirmations, procurement information, forecasts, and unresolved exceptions. Where a deadline cannot be met, the manager should communicate the expected delay and provide the information already available so Finance can plan accordingly. Repeated lateness should be analyzed to determine whether the problem is workload, unclear responsibility, inefficient workflow, or poor discipline. Finance should also avoid setting unrealistic deadlines or requesting information that is not actually needed. Good control requires both timely submissions and well-designed information requirements.

Study instruction:
- Map which delayed manager inputs affect the month-end close and identify the earliest date each is needed.

Key point:
- Timeliness is a financial-control requirement because late information affects reporting and decisions.
- Recurring delays should be addressed as process problems, not only individual failures.

Source links:
- COSO Monitoring Internal Control Systems
34. Q: How should managers learn to connect financial performance with project performance?
A: Show answer
Financial information becomes more useful when managers relate expenditure to what the project is actually delivering. A project may spend 80% of its budget and complete only 50% of planned outputs, which could require management attention. Another project may spend less than planned because implementation is delayed, while outputs remain achievable within the funding period. Managers should therefore review expenditure alongside activity milestones, beneficiary reach, procurement progress, staffing, and delivery schedules. Finance can support this by presenting financial data in the same project structure used by program teams. The goal is not to make Finance responsible for programme performance, but to make financial information useful in assessing whether resources are translating into planned results.

Study instruction:
- Pair financial indicators with operational indicators and explain what each combination tells management.

Key point:
- Spending should be interpreted alongside programme delivery.
- Financial reports become more useful when linked to operational results and milestones.

Source links:
- IFRC Project and Programme Planning Guidance Manual
- IFRC Monitoring and Evaluation Guide
35. Q: How should a checklist support escalation when a manager identifies a serious control problem?
A: Show answer
The checklist should provide a defined route for escalation instead of leaving managers to decide informally whom to contact. Serious issues may include suspected fraud, conflicts of interest, significant unauthorized spending, repeated deliberate circumvention of procedures, major donor ineligibility, material reporting errors, or significant asset-control concerns. The checklist should record the fact that an issue was identified and the responsible escalation route, while sensitive investigations should be handled through the organization’s approved process rather than documented in excessive detail in a widely shared operational file. Managers should know that escalation protects the organization and the programme; it is not simply a disciplinary mechanism. Finance, internal audit, senior management, safeguarding, legal, or other functions may have different responsibilities depending on the issue.

Study instruction:
- Review the organization’s escalation matrix and identify the correct route for several hypothetical control failures.

Key point:
- Serious exceptions require clear escalation routes and appropriate confidentiality.
- A checklist should help managers identify and route issues, not conduct sensitive investigations itself.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
36. Q: How should Finance measure whether non-finance managers are actually using financial information effectively?
A: Show answer
Training attendance alone does not demonstrate effective financial management. Finance should look for evidence of changed behavior: timely submission of forecasts, fewer repeated documentation errors, improved explanations of variances, earlier escalation of unusual transactions, fewer unsupported expenditures, more accurate commitments, and better linkage between budgets and operational decisions. Management review meetings can also track whether actions agreed from financial reports are completed. Where problems continue, Finance should determine whether the cause is knowledge, system design, unclear accountability, insufficient data, workload, or management incentives. A useful measure is therefore not “How many people completed training?” but “What financial-management behavior improved after training?” This creates a feedback loop between training design and control performance.

Study instruction:
- Define practical indicators that show whether managers are using financial information rather than merely receiving it.

Key point:
- Financial-management training should be evaluated by behavior and control outcomes, not attendance alone.
- Repeated errors should be analyzed for root causes.

Source links:
- COSO Monitoring Internal Control Systems
37. Q: How can financial checklists be designed so they do not become a paperwork burden?
A: Show answer
The checklist should contain only questions that support a decision, a control, a reporting requirement, or an evidence requirement. Each question should have a clear purpose and an identified owner. If Finance asks for information that does not change a decision or control outcome, the requirement should be reconsidered. The checklist can use conditional questions so users only complete sections relevant to the transaction. It can also rely on information already available in systems instead of asking managers to re-enter figures Finance already has. Periodic review should remove obsolete questions. A shorter checklist that managers complete thoughtfully is generally more useful than a long form that is treated as a routine signature exercise.

Study instruction:
- Review an existing checklist and challenge every question that does not lead to a defined action, evidence requirement, or control decision.

Key point:
- Good checklists are selective and purpose-driven.
- Reducing unnecessary data entry can improve control quality rather than weaken it.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
38. Q: How should Finance adapt the checklist to different types of managers?
A: Show answer
Not every manager needs the same financial information. A program manager may need budget, expenditure, commitments, forecast, donor restrictions, and activity-delivery information. A procurement manager may need commitments, supplier controls, authorization, and contract status. A country director may need consolidated financial risks, cash position, donor exposure, major variances, and management actions. The organization should therefore define a common core of controls and then add role-specific questions. This reduces duplication and makes the checklist more relevant. Training should explain the core responsibilities shared by all managers as well as the additional responsibilities connected to each role. Finance can use a common control framework while presenting different views of the information depending on the manager’s authority and decision needs.

Study instruction:
- Design a common core checklist and identify the role-specific sections required for three different management positions.

Key point:
- Control principles can be standardized while information needs remain role-specific.
- Relevance improves the quality of managerial review.

Source links:
- COSO Internal Control Guidance
39. Q: How should checklists be integrated into management reporting rather than treated as separate forms?
A: Show answer
A checklist is most effective when its outputs feed management reporting. For example, unresolved documentation issues, forecast changes, pending approvals, significant variances, and control exceptions can become part of the monthly management report. This avoids creating an additional administrative process that disappears after the form is submitted. Finance can use a structured checklist to identify exceptions and summarize them by project, manager, donor, risk, or action status. Managers then see a direct connection between completing the checklist and the information used in management discussions. The reporting process should preserve the evidence behind important exceptions while keeping the dashboard or management report concise enough to support decisions. The checklist is the control input; management reporting is one of the ways the resulting information is used.

Study instruction:
- Trace several checklist responses into a sample monthly management report and identify how they affect decisions.

Key point:
- Checklist information becomes more valuable when it feeds existing management processes.
- Avoid duplicating information across disconnected forms and reports.

Source links:
- COSO Monitoring Internal Control Systems
40. Q: How should Finance train managers to understand financial information while preserving their operational role?
A: Show answer
The training should repeatedly reinforce a simple model: managers own operational decisions and the facts surrounding implementation; Finance owns the accounting framework and technical financial reporting; both functions share responsibility for timely, accurate information. Training can use realistic cases where managers review a budget, identify a variance, confirm an activity, consider a donor restriction, and decide when to escalate. The emphasis should be on asking good questions rather than memorizing accounting terminology. Managers should know what the numbers mean, what action the numbers may require, what information they need to provide, and when a technical question belongs with Finance. This produces a practical control culture in which financial management becomes part of programme management without turning programme staff into substitute accountants.

Study instruction:
- Practice the complete cycle: read the financial information, identify the operational issue, confirm the evidence, determine the action, and escalate technical matters to Finance.

Key point:
- Effective training connects financial information to managerial action while preserving technical accounting responsibility within Finance.
- The strongest checklist culture is based on shared accountability, clear roles, evidence, and timely escalation.

- COSO Internal Control Guidance

Table 8
Staff Advance Controls
1. Q: How should staff advances be accounted for until they are properly settled?
A: Show answer
A staff advance should generally be maintained as an outstanding receivable or other staff-related asset when the organization has provided cash to an employee who is required to account for the funds, submit eligible supporting evidence, return unused cash, or otherwise clear the amount under the organization’s policy and applicable reporting framework. The initial payment is therefore not automatically an expense simply because cash has left the bank. The accounting records should distinguish the advance from the later approved expenditure. When the employee submits acceptable documentation, finance can reclassify the supported amount to the appropriate expense, asset, or other account and clear any remaining amount through cash return or reimbursement. The balance should stay visible until the underlying obligation is resolved.

Study instruction:
- Understand the difference between issuing cash and recognizing the final expense.

Key point:
- An outstanding staff advance should remain visible until it is properly cleared.
- Final expense recognition should follow approved settlement evidence and policy.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — Conceptual Framework
- IPSASB — Public Sector Conceptual Framework
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
2. Q: What is a staff advance in practical NGO accounting terms?
A: Show answer
A staff advance is cash provided to an employee before the final expenditure has been fully documented and approved. Common examples include travel advances, field-activity advances, workshop advances, emergency operational advances, and small project-related advances. The important accounting feature is that the employee has not yet demonstrated how the funds were ultimately used. The organization therefore needs a temporary balance that can be monitored until settlement. The register should identify the staff member, transaction reference, project, purpose, date issued, amount issued, settlement due date, amount supported, amount returned, amount reimbursed, and outstanding balance. A clear definition helps finance avoid mixing advances with completed expenses and makes the control objective easy to communicate to program and operations staff.

Study instruction:
- Be able to explain what event creates the advance and what event clears it.

Key point:
- The advance is a temporary accounting balance linked to a staff member and purpose.
- The settlement process converts the temporary balance into approved final accounting entries.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — Conceptual Framework
- IPSASB — Public Sector Conceptual Framework
- Microsoft Learn — Power Query Documentation
3. Q: Why should a staff advance not be posted immediately as an expense?
A: Show answer
At the time cash is issued, the organization may not yet know whether the employee will spend the full amount, whether every payment will be eligible, whether any amount will be returned, or whether the final transaction belongs to the original project code. Posting the entire amount as an expense at issue can therefore overstate expenditure and understate the outstanding staff balance. The stronger process is to record the advance separately, then process the settlement after receipts, activity evidence, approvals, and cash-return documentation have been reviewed. The exact journal sequence should follow the organization’s accounting policy and reporting framework, but the control principle is the same: cash movement and final expense recognition should not be treated as identical events.

Study instruction:
- Focus on the accounting distinction between cash disbursement and final cost recognition.

Key point:
- Cash leaving the bank does not by itself prove the final expense classification.
- Settlement evidence determines how the advance is ultimately cleared.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — Conceptual Framework
- IPSASB — Public Sector Conceptual Framework
4. Q: What information should a staff advance register contain?
A: Show answer
The register should contain enough information to answer five questions: who received the funds, why were they issued, how much was issued, what has been accounted for, and what remains open. Core fields should include staff ID or controlled staff reference, staff name, advance reference, advance date, purpose, project or grant code, department, currency, amount issued, settlement due date, amount acquitted, amount returned, amount reimbursed to the employee, outstanding balance, settlement date, reviewer, approval status, and aging status. It can also include document-completeness fields for authorization, payment proof, activity evidence, receipts, settlement form, and cash-return evidence. Keeping these fields in a structured table makes the register useful for both accounting review and management reporting rather than merely acting as a list of unpaid advances.

Study instruction:
- Build a register that supports transaction tracing, aging, reconciliation, and follow-up.

Key point:
- Every open balance needs an owner, amount, purpose, due date, and status.
- Supporting-document fields turn the register into a control schedule.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
5. Q: How should the outstanding balance of each staff advance be calculated?
A: Show answer
A simple control formula is to start with the amount issued and reduce it by amounts that have been validly accounted for or returned, while separately recognizing any approved reimbursement due to the employee where the policy requires it. The exact formula should match the organization’s settlement workflow so that a single payment is not deducted twice. For example, the schedule can maintain separate columns for amount issued, eligible expenditure approved, cash returned, and reimbursement due, with a final control balance calculated from those components. A separate status field should indicate whether the balance is open, partially settled, fully settled, disputed, or under review. This is safer than allowing staff to overwrite one “balance” cell manually because every movement remains visible and auditable.

Study instruction:
- Make the balance formula transparent enough that another reviewer can reproduce it.

Key point:
- The balance should be calculated from documented movements, not typed manually.
- Separate settlement components reduce double-counting and hidden adjustments.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Object Model
6. Q: Why is aging an essential control for staff advances?
A: Show answer
Aging turns an outstanding balance into a management signal. A register showing only the total amount open does not tell management whether balances are recent and normal or whether a small number of old items are becoming control problems. Finance should calculate days outstanding from the advance date or, where policy requires, from the settlement due date. The schedule can then group balances into categories such as current, 1–30 days overdue, 31–60, 61–90, and over 90 days, with thresholds adjusted to the organization’s policy. Aging should not replace investigation; it tells the reviewer where investigation should start. Power Query can derive the age consistently each refresh, making the overdue population easier to identify without recalculating every record manually.

Study instruction:
- Practice distinguishing total outstanding value from the age and risk profile of that value.

Key point:
- Aging shows which balances need attention first.
- The organization’s policy should define due dates and escalation thresholds.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- IFRS Foundation — Conceptual Framework
7. Q: What is the difference between current and overdue staff advances?
A: Show answer
A current advance is still within the approved settlement period, while an overdue advance has passed the applicable due date without complete and accepted settlement. The distinction should be based on a documented policy date rather than informal judgment. An advance may be old but not overdue if the approved activity runs for a longer period and the policy provides an appropriate settlement date. Conversely, a smaller advance can become a control issue quickly if it remains unresolved after its due date. The register should therefore use both age and status. Management reporting should show the total current balance separately from overdue balances and, where useful, identify the oldest items and the responsible staff member or manager. This allows follow-up to be proportionate and evidence-based.

Study instruction:
- Use both due-date status and age when evaluating outstanding advances.

Key point:
- Overdue status should come from approved rules, not guesswork.
- Age and status together provide a clearer risk picture.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — Conceptual Framework
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
8. Q: How should an NGO follow up overdue staff advances?
A: Show answer
Follow-up should be structured rather than informal. The first step is to identify the item, responsible employee, due date, balance, project, and reason for non-settlement. Finance can then request the missing documentation or cash return and record the action taken. Escalation should follow policy and may involve the employee’s manager, project lead, HR, or senior management when the balance remains unresolved. The control log should capture reminder date, response received, promised settlement date, and eventual resolution. Repeated delays should be analyzed separately because the issue may be a process weakness, unclear travel rules, slow review, inadequate training, or a broader compliance concern. VBA can prepare reminder lists automatically, while the human reviewer remains responsible for deciding what action is appropriate.

Study instruction:
- Learn to combine automated reminders with documented management follow-up.

Key point:
- Every overdue item should have a visible follow-up history.
- Repeated delays should trigger process analysis, not just repeated reminders.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
9. Q: What control should apply before a new advance is issued to a staff member with an old balance?
A: Show answer
A new advance should be checked against the organization’s advance policy before approval. The reviewer should determine whether the employee has an existing unsettled balance, whether the new activity is genuinely separate, whether the policy permits multiple advances, and whether the manager has documented an exception where one is allowed. The test should be automated where practical, but exceptions should remain subject to human approval. A useful workbook control is a pre-issue status field that checks the staff master and current advance register and returns statuses such as “No open advance,” “Open balance,” “Overdue,” or “Policy exception required.” This makes the rule visible before cash leaves the bank and reduces the chance of accumulating overlapping advances without management awareness.

Study instruction:
- Design pre-payment controls that prevent new exposure from being created before old exposure is reviewed.

Key point:
- Open balances should be visible before new advances are approved.
- Policy exceptions should be documented rather than silently bypassing the control.

Source links:
- Microsoft Support — Apply Data Validation to Cells
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
10. Q: Why should advance authorization be retained as supporting evidence?
A: Show answer
Authorization establishes that the advance was approved for a legitimate organizational purpose before the payment was made. The evidence should normally identify the employee, purpose, project or activity, amount or approved limit, approving person, date, and any relevant policy basis. Finance can then connect the authorization to the actual payment and later settlement. This creates a clear chain from approval to disbursement to accountability. Missing authorization makes it difficult to determine whether an advance was validly issued even when receipts are eventually submitted. The register can therefore include a document-status field that distinguishes “authorization received,” “payment evidence received,” “settlement evidence received,” and “cash return evidence received.” The objective is to make the control trail complete without relying on email memory or verbal confirmation.

Study instruction:
- Trace each advance from authorization through settlement using identifiable evidence.

Key point:
- Authorization proves that the advance was approved before issue.
- Supporting evidence should connect approval, payment, and final settlement.

Source links:
- IPSASB — Public Sector Conceptual Framework
- IFRS Foundation — Conceptual Framework
11. Q: What supporting documents should normally accompany the settlement of a staff advance?
A: Show answer
The evidence should be sufficient to demonstrate what activity occurred, what was purchased or consumed, what amount was spent, who approved it, and what happened to any unused funds. Depending on the type of advance, this may include the authorization, payment voucher or bank evidence, travel authorization, itinerary or activity record, attendance or event documentation, supplier receipts, invoices, signed settlement forms, and evidence that unused cash was returned. The exact documents should follow the organization’s policy and donor requirements. The register should not simply mark an item “supported”; it can identify missing evidence by category so reviewers know exactly what remains outstanding. This improves follow-up and helps distinguish an accounting classification issue from a documentation problem.

Study instruction:
- Review settlements by evidence category rather than relying on a single completion flag.

Key point:
- A complete settlement needs evidence of both expenditure and unused-fund disposition.
- Missing-document analysis makes exceptions easier to resolve.

Source links:
- IPSASB — Public Sector Conceptual Framework
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
12. Q: How should the staff advance register be reconciled to the general ledger?
A: Show answer
Reconciliation should compare the total outstanding balance in the detailed register with the corresponding general-ledger control account or accounts at a defined reporting date. The reviewer should investigate differences caused by timing, unposted settlements, duplicate entries, staff or project coding errors, foreign exchange, manual journals, or transactions recorded in the ledger but missing from the register. A useful reconciliation sheet should show opening balance, new advances, settlements, returns, adjustments, closing balance per register, closing balance per general ledger, and the difference. Every difference should have a documented explanation and resolution status. Power Query can consolidate the register and ledger extracts, while VBA can generate a reconciliation summary and highlight non-zero differences for review.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile the detailed listing to the control account every reporting period and explain every difference.

Key point:
- A detailed schedule is not complete until it agrees to the general ledger or documented reconciliation differences.
- Reconciliation should be performed at a consistent cutoff date.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Object Model
13. Q: Why should the advance register also be reconciled to bank or cash records?
A: Show answer
The general ledger confirms the accounting record, but bank and cash evidence confirms that the actual disbursement and return movements occurred. For example, a register could contain an advance recorded as issued even though the payment was rejected or reversed by the bank. It could also omit a cash return that has already reached the bank account. A complete control process therefore connects the advance reference to the payment transaction and any subsequent return of unused cash. Power Query can standardize bank and cashbook references and compare them to the advance register. Exceptions should be reviewed rather than automatically forced to match, because timing differences and batching can create legitimate differences that require interpretation.

Study instruction:
- Trace major cash movements back to the register and supporting payment evidence.

Key point:
- Accounting records and cash records should support the same transaction story.
- Automated matching should identify exceptions rather than replace review.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
14. Q: How should partially settled staff advances be controlled?
A: Show answer
A partial settlement occurs when some expenditure has been accepted but an amount remains outstanding. The workbook should preserve the original issue amount and show every subsequent settlement movement separately rather than replacing the original balance. For example, a staff member may submit approved receipts for one portion of the advance and return unused cash later. Finance should record each component with its own date, reference, and approval status. The remaining amount should continue aging until fully cleared. Partial settlements are a common area for errors because users may assume that submitting a form closes the whole advance. A structured register makes the remaining balance visible and allows the next reviewer to continue from the prior position without rebuilding the calculation.

Study instruction:
- Treat partial settlement as a change in balance, not as automatic closure.

Key point:
- Each settlement component should remain traceable.
- An advance is closed only when the full balance is appropriately cleared.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
15. Q: How should unused cash returned by a staff member be reflected in the records?
A: Show answer
When a staff member returns unused cash, the return should be linked directly to the outstanding advance and supported by evidence such as a cash-receipt record, deposit slip, bank transaction, or other approved proof. The returned amount should reduce the advance balance rather than being treated as unrelated income. The finance team should match the return to the original advance reference so that the employee’s balance is cleared accurately. Where cash is returned in a different currency or through a bank account that creates timing differences, the organization’s applicable accounting policy should determine the precise treatment of any resulting difference. The register should retain the return date, amount, currency, reference, and evidence status so that a reviewer can confirm how the cash movement cleared the exposure.

Study instruction:
- Link every cash return to the specific advance it settles.

Key point:
- Returned funds reduce the outstanding advance rather than becoming operating income merely because cash came back.
- Cash-return evidence should be retained and matched to the original transaction.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — Conceptual Framework
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
16. Q: What should happen when a staff member spends more than the advance amount?
A: Show answer
An employee may incur eligible costs above the amount originally advanced. The settlement should first establish the amount of eligible expenditure supported by acceptable evidence. The organization can then determine whether the excess amount is properly reimbursable under policy or whether it requires additional approval. The original advance remains a separate component of the settlement calculation. A reimbursement due to the employee should not be confused with an increase in the original advance balance. The workbook should therefore show approved expenditure, original advance, excess eligible expenditure, reimbursement amount, and final status. This makes it clear whether the employee owes money to the organization, the organization owes money to the employee, or the transaction is fully settled.

Study instruction:
- Separate the settlement of the original advance from any later reimbursement obligation.

Key point:
- An excess claim and an outstanding advance are different control questions.
- Eligibility and approval should be established before reimbursement is processed.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — Conceptual Framework
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
17. Q: How should staff advances be linked to projects and grants?
A: Show answer
The advance register should record the approved project, grant, cost centre, or activity code at the time the advance is authorized. The settlement should then confirm that the actual expenditure belongs to the same funding purpose or, where permitted, document an approved reclassification. This is especially important in NGOs because an advance may be issued for a broad operational purpose but later contain expenditures that span several activities. The workbook should not simply accept the original project code as proof that every final cost belongs there. Settlement review should validate the coding against the activity evidence and funding restrictions. Power Query can compare project codes with controlled master lists, while exception reports can identify missing or invalid codes before the final journal is posted.

Study instruction:
- Trace project coding from advance approval through final settlement and posting.

Key point:
- The original code is an allocation expectation, not automatic proof of final eligibility.
- Final project classification should be supported by the settlement evidence and policy.

18. Q: Why is project-code validation important before an advance is issued?
A: Show answer
A project code that is inactive, misspelled, or incorrectly selected can cause the entire advance to appear in the wrong program or grant. The control should therefore validate the proposed project code against a maintained master list that includes status, funding source, responsible manager, and where appropriate, approved activity dates. A rejected or inactive code should be flagged before payment rather than corrected later through manual journal adjustments. Excel data validation can restrict input to approved values, and Power Query can refresh the master-data relationships from a controlled source. The key is that validation supports classification accuracy but does not replace the underlying budget and authorization review. The financial officer should still confirm that the purpose and amount are appropriate before approving the advance.

Study instruction:
- Use controlled master data to prevent invalid project classifications at source.

Key point:
- Data validation reduces avoidable coding errors.
- Correct coding still depends on proper approval and purpose review.

19. Q: How can an advance register identify duplicate staff advances?
A: Show answer
Duplicate detection should use a transaction key rather than simply comparing staff names and amounts. Useful fields may include advance reference, staff identifier, payment date, project, purpose, amount, and bank or cash transaction reference. A workbook can flag exact duplicates and near-duplicates separately. An exact duplicate may indicate a repeated import or duplicate posting, while a near-duplicate may be legitimate separate activity requiring review. Power Query can remove duplicates where the source process guarantees a unique key, or retain them as exceptions where a human decision is necessary. The control should never delete a record merely because it looks similar. The reviewer should confirm whether the apparent duplicate represents the same transaction, a correction, or two genuine payments.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish duplicate data from legitimate repeated transactions before taking corrective action.

Key point:
- Duplicate detection is a review control, not automatic evidence of error.
- Unique references are the strongest basis for transaction matching.

- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?

20. Q: How should staff master data support advance controls?
A: Show answer
The advance process depends on accurate staff identification. The master list should contain a controlled staff identifier, staff name, department, manager, employment status, and other fields needed by the organization’s policy. Using a stable ID is safer than relying on a typed name because names can be similar, change, or contain inconsistent spelling. When an employee leaves or transfers, the master should update their status and responsible manager so open advances remain visible. Power Query can join the advance register to the staff master and bring in current status and management information. The system should protect sensitive staff information and limit access to those who need it. Data accuracy and privacy are therefore both part of the control design.

Study instruction:
- Use stable identifiers and controlled staff master data when building advance schedules.

Key point:
- Staff IDs provide more reliable matching than free-text names.
- Staff-data access should be limited according to organizational requirements.
